--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This is the title page of an influential alchemical collection printed in Strasbourg by Lazarus Zetzner. The work is attributed to Geber, the Latinized name of the 8th-century polymath Jabir ibn Hayyan, though many of these specific texts are now believed to have been written by a 13th-century European author known as "Pseudo-Geber."</meta>

### OF GEBER THE ARAB,
# THE PHILOSOPHER AND ALCHEMIST,
## MOST ACUTE,
### CONCERNING ALCHEMY

->*The Tradition of Supreme Perfection <note>original: "Traditio summae perfectionis." This is one of the most famous and influential texts in the history of Western alchemy, providing instructions on the preparation of the "Elixir" or Philosopher's Stone.</note> divided into two books.*<-

->Also:<-

->*The Book of Investigation into the Mastery <term>Mastery: From the Latin "magisterium," referring to the successful completion of an alchemical process or the creation of the transmuting agent itself.</term> by the same author.*<-

->Purged of the countless errors with which it formerly swarmed, and restored to its original order.<-

A woodcut printer's device featuring a bust of a woman (Minerva or a personification of Knowledge) in profile, facing left. She wears a highly decorative plumed helmet and a pearl necklace. The bust is set upon a simple square stone pedestal. Flanking the image is the vertical text: "K N O W L E D G E" <note>original: "SCIENTIA"</note> on the left and "U N C H A N G E A B L E" <note>original: "IMMUTABILIS"</note> on the right.

->At the expense of LAZARUS ZETZNER <note>Lazarus Zetzner (d. 1616) was a prominent publisher in Strasbourg who specialized in monumental collections of alchemical and philosophical texts, most notably the "Theatrum Chemicum."</note>,<-
->1598 <note>original: "CIƆ IƆ XCIIX," an older form of Roman numeral notation for 1598.</note><-

-><insert>1 5 9 8</insert><-

<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the work, following the "bleed-through" title page seen previously. The recipient, Michael Theurer, is a specific political figure in Strasbourg, and the text begins the author's justification for publishing a work on alchemy, acknowledging the controversy surrounding the subject.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRI-
# OUS AND MOST PRU-
## DENT MAN, MOST RENOWNED IN VIRTUE,
## LEARNING, AND MERIT,
### Master MICHAEL THEURER, of the
### renowned Republic of Strasbourg, Member
### of the Council of Fifteen <note>original: "Quindecimviro." The Council of Fifteen (Fünfzehner) was one of the three inner executive councils that governed the Free Imperial City of Strasbourg.</note>, etc. To his lord, patron,
### and protector, most highly to be respected, [the author] sends greetings.

*I have no doubt,*
*most illustrious and*
*most prudent man, that*
*concerning the publication*
*of this little book <note>original: "libelli." Authors often used this modest term to refer to their published works regardless of length.</note>, prepared at my*
*own expense at this time, there*
*will be many and diverse*
*judgments from many people.*
*For indeed, there will not be*
*lacking those to whom it will*
*seem too absurd, or rather*
*foolish and inept, to commend the*
<term>Chemical art</term> <note>The "art of Chemistry" here refers to alchemy, which in the 16th and 17th centuries was a blend of spiritual philosophy and practical laboratory science.</note> *to others with words: as something which [arises] from the highest uncer-*

<meta>This page continues the author's introductory defense of alchemy, building on the previous page's mention of the "highest uncertainty." The catchword "mæ" at the bottom of the page indicates the completion of the word "animae" (soul) on the following page.</meta>

[uncer-]tainty and the worst deceptions <note>original: "imposturis"</note> it has been suspected for so many centuries: and whose end <note>The "end" or goal refers to the completion of the "Great Work," such as the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</note>, for the sake of which especially all labor in it is undertaken, no one as far as is known, or certainly only the very fewest, have ever attained. There will also be those who will cry out that they sin against the public good—those who set out to divulge this art through published books and, in this manner, to cultivate and propagate it. For many believe that it serves greed <note>original: "avaritiae"</note>, and that it so entices the minds of wicked men with beautiful allurements and the hope of riches <note>original: "divitiarum"</note>, with which they might satisfy their lust, that they rush blindly, as it were, to their ruin: and not only of their fortunes, but also of their reputation and health <note>original: "valetudinis"</note>, and not rarely even of their sou-

<summary>The author addresses the common criticisms of alchemy, acknowledging its reputation for deception and the way the hope of wealth can lead the greedy to financial and physical ruin.</summary>

<meta>This page completes the word "souls" (animae) from the previous page and continues the author's exploration of common arguments against alchemy. The author is listing the grievances of critics who believe the art produces only criminals and fraudsters.</meta>

-ls suffer loss. For who,
they say, will deny—except
for certain **smoke-sellers** <term>smoke-sellers: from the Latin *fumivendulos*, referring to charlatans who sell empty promises or "hot air"</term>, who, being themselves most destitute, promise to others **mountains of gold** and immense riches with speech marvelously composed for fraud—[who will deny] that most skillful brewers of poisons <note>Alchemy was often criticized because the same chemical knowledge used to seek the Elixir could be used to create deadly toxins.</note>, **counterfeiters of coins**, and indeed an infinite multitude of the most perverse men emerge from the workshop <note>original: *officinâ*, referring to the alchemist's laboratory</note> of this art? Therefore, this art is not to be recommended to others, much less spread and propagated through published books; but it is to be utterly cast out and uprooted from all well-constituted **commonwealths** <note>original: *rebus publicis*</note>, and even from the fellowship of all men. To whose judgment I certainly a-
<meta>catchword: a- (likely beginning "assentior" or "agree")</meta>

<summary>The author details the harsh criticisms of alchemy, noting its reputation for producing counterfeiters, poisoners, and "smoke-selling" charlatans who promise wealth while being poor themselves. Critics argue the practice should be banned from all civilized society.</summary>

<keywords>smoke-sellers, counterfeiters, poisoners, fraud, mountains of gold, commonwealth, social exile, alchemical workshop</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page, completing the Latin word "aliquid" (something/some weight). The author is transitioning from acknowledging the critics' points to defending the inherent value of alchemy when practiced correctly.</meta>

—weight <note>original: *aliquid tribuerem*—literally "I would grant something" or "I would give some weight to their opinion"</note>; and I would willingly subscribe to such a severe judgment, if they were recounting to me the vices of the art itself, and not of the men who abuse the art. 

But truly, if we set aside their slanders and consider what great benefits have reached every part of human life by the grace of this art, we cannot help but hold it in high regard. For, setting aside those uses it provides to **Medicine** <term>Medicine: the author refers to "Iatrochemistry," the use of alchemy to create purified chemical medicines</term>, it teaches how to track down the hidden **forms** <note>In a philosophical sense, the "form" is the essential nature or power of a substance, as opposed to its mere physical matter.</note> of things, to refine—so to speak—**coarse bodies**, to separate the pure from the impure within them, and to clothe them in an incorruptible form, so that they might exert their powers without the hindrance of a coarse and impure body. Does not **brass** <note>original: *aurichalcum* (orichalcum); a golden-colored alloy, usually brass, which alchemists cited as proof of their ability to transform the appearance and properties of metals.</note>—

<meta>catchword: cum (with/when)</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while critics rightly condemn the men who abuse alchemy, the art itself is noble and beneficial, particularly for its ability to purify substances and reveal their hidden medicinal powers.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy vs. alchemists, purification, coarse bodies, Medicine, form and matter, brass, orichalcum, pharmaceutical chemistry</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the rhetorical question from the previous page regarding the practical benefits of alchemy, specifically following the mention of "brass" (orichalcum). It transitions into a defense of the art against its critics.</meta>

<sig>A 4</sig>

*—with the mixtures of metals, solders, assays, and **separations** <term>Separations: chemical processes used to isolate specific elements or substances from a mixture, a fundamental technique in both alchemy and modern chemistry.</term> that we owe to this art? Has not the most useful craft of **glassmaking** <note>original: *vitri conficiendi*; alchemical research into salts and high-heat furnaces was essential for the development of clear, high-quality glass.</note>, along with countless other arts which human life cannot comfortably do without at this time, emerged from this art? To this I would almost dare to affirm that this art has no hater except the ignorant, the **ill-disposed** <note>original: *malè feriatos*—literally "badly on holiday" or "idle," referring to those who have nothing better to do than disparage the hard work of others.</note>, and those who are accustomed to approve of nothing except what they themselves do; and who, with a certain **dog-like envy** <note>original: *caninâ... invidiâ*; a reference to the "dog in the manger" fable, describing someone who prevents others from enjoying something they cannot use or understand themselves.</note>, strive to keep others away from the **sustenance** <note>original: *pabulo*—literally "fodder" or "pasture," here used metaphorically for the intellectual or material fruits of alchemical knowledge.</note> which they themselves are not permitted to enjoy. Which fact indeed ought to make this art even more commendable in a way. For as for what is said concerning the abuse, imposture, and evil practitioners—* <note>The text cuts off mid-word: *dicunt* (they say/is said).</note>

<summary>The author lists the practical technologies derived from alchemy, such as metallurgy and glassmaking, and argues that the art's only enemies are the ignorant and the envious.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, glassmaking, assays, separations, dog-like envy, defense of alchemy, practical arts, imposture</keywords>

*dly claim the foremost place.*
*This short work of this author concerning the*
**TRADITION OF THE HIGHEST PERFE-** <note>original: *Traditione Summae Perfectionis*; this is the title of one of the most influential alchemical books in the Western world, providing clear instructions for laboratory processes.</note>

judged it right: I wished to specifically inscribe and dedicate to **YOU** this labor—which, while indeed the work of another <note>original: *laborem alienum*; the editor refers to the fact that the text was written by the medieval alchemist Geber, not himself</note>, has yet in some way become my own through my own expenses—so that I might not only have a patron against those who might too harshly judge my undertaking in publishing this little work <note>original: *opusculo*; a "little work," a common modest term for a book</note>: but also so that I might, in this manner, offer at least some slight sign of a grateful soul in return for Your singular benevolence and favor, so often already declared toward me. Therefore, may you receive this gift <term>gift: the Latin *munusculum* literally means a "small gift," often used when a writer presents a book to a patron</term>—slight indeed, but proceeding from a dutiful spirit—with that same benevolence with which you have embraced me until now: and indeed, hereafter continue your accustomed kindness toward all

# TABLE OF CONTENTS.

Preface. CHAPTER I. page 1.
Division of the Book. CHAPTER II. 4
**FIRST PART OF THE FIRST BOOK.** Concerning the obstacles to this art <note>original: *artis*; here "art" refers specifically to the alchemical art of transmutation</note>. CHAPTER III. 5
Concerning the obstacles to the work on the part of the craftsman's <term>craftsman: the Latin *artifex* refers to the alchemist or practitioner of the chemical arts</term> body. CHAPTER IV. 6
Concerning the obstacles on the part of the craftsman's soul <note>original: *animae*; referring to the psychological or spiritual state of the alchemist</note>. CHAPTER V. 7
Concerning external obstacles hindering the work. CHAPTER VI. 9
Epilogue: what kind of person the craftsman ought to be. CHAPTER VII. 11
**SECOND PART OF THIS BOOK**, in which the arguments of those who deny this art are presented, and then refuted. CHAPTER VIII. 15
Arguments that motivate those who simply deny this art. CHAPTER IX. 16
The objection: that it can neither happen nor ought it to happen, that art should imitate nature in all differences of properties. CHAPTER X. 22
Refutation of the arguments of those who simply deny the art. CHAPTER XI. 25
Division of the opinions of those who suppose that the art exists. CHAPTER XII. 35

# CLEAR AND MANIFEST
# PROOF OF THE
# CHEMICAL ART.

### Taken from the *Antiquities* of Petrus Apianus. <note>Petrus Apianus (1495–1552) was a German humanist, astronomer, and mathematician. His *Inscriptiones sacrosanctae vetustatis* (1534) was a famous collection of ancient inscriptions.</note>

At Padua some years ago, a wonderful evidence of the alchemical art <note>original: *Alchymicæ artis*</note> was discovered.

There was an earthenware urn, inscribed with these verses:

> *Thieves, do not touch this gift sacred to Pluto:* <note>Pluto was the Roman god of the underworld, often associated with the metals and hidden treasures of the earth.</note>
> *That which lies hidden in this urn is unknown to you.*
> *For Maximus Olibus enclosed the elements, arranged with heavy labor,*
> *under this modest vessel.*
> *May Abundance with her fertile horn stand guard,* <note>original: *copia cornu*; referring to the cornucopia.</note>
> *Lest the value of such a great liquid be lost.*

Inside this urn another smaller one was found; when it was opened, two skillfully crafted vials <term>vials: the original Latin *ampulla* refers to a small jar or flask, often used in ancient Rome for perfumes or oils.</term> were discovered; one of silver, the other of gold, full of a certain very pure liquid, by which a lamp is thought to have been kept still burning for many centuries. <note>This refers to the legend of "ever-burning lamps," which alchemists often cited as proof of a "perpetual fire" or a perfected substance that does not consume itself.</note>

These verses were engraved upon the small urn:

> *Depart from here, you wicked thieves!*
> *What do you want with your prying eyes?*
> *Depart from here with your Mercury, wearer of the traveler's hat and bearer of the staff:* <note>original: *Mercurio petasato caduceoque*. Mercury was the god of thieves, but in this context, he is the patron of alchemy. The *petasus* is his winged hat and the *caduceus* is his snaked-entwined staff.</note>
> *Maximus makes this sacred gift to the greatest Pluto.*

<summary>This page recounts the legendary discovery of a Roman funerary urn in Padua containing gold and silver vials of a mysterious liquid. This "ever-burning lamp" is presented as historical evidence for the reality and longevity of alchemical secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Padua, Maximus Olibus, ever-burning lamp, Pluto, Mercury, archaeological proof, liquid gold, liquid silver</keywords>

# THE TRADITION OF THE SUMMARY OF PERFECTION IN ALCHEMY BY GEBER THE ARAB. <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815 AD). While traditionally attributed to him, modern scholars believe this specific text, the *Summa Perfectionis*, was likely written in the 13th century by a European author known as Pseudo-Geber.</note>

## DIVIDED INTO TWO BOOKS.

## THE FIRST BOOK, WHICH CONTAINS FOUR PARTS.

->*Preface*<-

### CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'T' featuring floral vine motifs and a background sketch of a landscape with a building.</image-desc>

We have here reduced into one summary <note>original: *summam*</note> all of our knowledge regarding the science of transmuting <term>transmuting: the alchemical process of changing one substance into another, specifically "base" metals like lead into "noble" metals like gold.</term> metals, which we have abridged from the books of the philosophers of old and compiled in various ways throughout our own volumes. Furthermore, those things which exist in an incomplete state <note>original: *diminuta*</note> in other books written by us, we have in this [work] of highest perfection...

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the "Summa Perfectionis," where the author (Geber) announces his intention to consolidate and complete his previous teachings on the transmutation of metals into one comprehensive summary.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of the "Summa Perfectionis." The author explains his method of "hiding" and "revealing" information across different parts of the text, a common alchemical trope used to ensure only the "worthy" (the prudent) could understand the secrets. The catchword "faci-" at the bottom of the previous page would have led into the first word of the full text block here.</meta>

<header>BOOK ONE</header>

...by delivery <note>original: *traditione*; here referring to the formal handing down of knowledge</note>, we have compensated for, and their deficiency we have supplied in a brief discourse. Likewise, that which was hidden by us in one part, we have made manifest elsewhere within that same part of this our volume. Thus, the completion of such an excellent and noble part of philosophy may be revealed to the prudent.

Know therefore, dearest son, that in this volume, the operation of this art is sufficiently contained in general chapters, universally and without any omission. By God, he who works according to this book will rejoice to have found the true end of this art.

But know, most dear one, that he who is ignorant of the natural principles in <term>philosophy: here meaning "natural philosophy," the study of nature and the physical universe that was the precursor to modern science</term> is far removed from this art of ours, since he does not have the true root upon which to found his intention.

He, however, who understands the natural principles and the causes of <term>minerals: the inorganic substances of the earth, which alchemists studied to understand the "growth" and maturation of metals</term>, is close. Nevertheless, he has not yet attained the true root or the profitable end of this most hidden art, although he may have...

<summary>In this passage, the author claims to have completed and clarified previous alchemical teachings, emphasizing that success in the "art" requires a deep understanding of natural philosophy and the origins of minerals.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Philosophy, Alchemical Operation, Mineral Causes, Hidden Art, Scientific Principles, Geber</keywords>

### PART TWO

...has an easier access to the principles of this art than one whose mind is clouded by ignorance <note>original: *ignorantia ſuper mentẽ*; literally "ignorance falling upon the mind"</note> concerning the method and progress of this our work; and know that such a man is but a little way from the entrance of the art. 

Truly, he who understands all the principles of nature and the causes of <term>minerals: in this context, the inorganic substances of the earth, particularly metals, which alchemists believed "grew" within the earth over long periods</term>, as well as the manner of their generation—which consists of the <term>intention: the inherent purpose or directed goal of a natural process</term> of nature—only a small thing is withheld from him regarding the completion of the work, without which our science cannot be perfected. This is because <term>Art: the human practice of alchemy</term> is not able to follow nature in all its operations, but rather imitates it as far as it can.

Therefore, dearest son, we reveal that secret to you, for in this those seeking this art go astray: because they desire to imitate nature in every specific property of its action <note>The author suggests that alchemists fail when they try to copy nature's slow, geological timeline exactly, rather than using art to accelerate or refine the process</note>. Labor, therefore, to study our volumes, especially this book, and revolve our sayings very often in your heart, so that you may draw out the true intention from our manner of speaking. For in these you will find the foundation upon which you may base your intention, and you shall know...

### CHAPTER II

First, therefore, we shall briefly set forth all the obstacles by which the <term>practitioner: the alchemist or 'artifex' performing the work</term> is hindered in this work, preventing him from reaching the true end. And finally, we shall speak of those things which it is expedient and necessary for the practitioner of this work to possess within himself <note>Alchemical texts often emphasized the moral and intellectual character of the "Artifex" as a prerequisite for success</note>.

Secondly, we shall dispute against the ignorant and the <term>sophists: here referring to those who use false reasoning or fraudulent methods</term>, who, because of their own ignorance and inability while seeking the mastery and profit of this art, destroy the reputation of the art and affirm that it does not exist. Therefore, we shall first set down all their reasons and persuasions, and afterward we shall most clearly destroy them, so that it may be sufficiently plain to the prudent that the arguments of those sophists contain no part of the truth.

Thirdly, we shall dispute concerning the natural principles which pertain to the <term>intention: the inherent purpose or directed goal of a natural process</term> of nature, and likewise concerning the manner <note>original: *modo*</note>...

Finally, there are those who are slaves to money and greed; they desire to discover this art, and though they claim to support it, they are afraid to provide the necessary <note>original: 'impensas', referring to the financial costs of equipment and materials</note> expenses. Because of this greed for money, they never reach their goal. Our science, therefore, does not reach any of these people. For how can someone reach it if they are ignorant of it and neglect to investigate it?

It is now clear from what has been said what the obstacles are in this art that hold one back and prevent anyone from reaching its final goal.

### *An epilogue on the qualities required of the practitioner.*
## CHAPTER VII.

We conclude, therefore, from the aforementioned things, that the <term>practitioner: 'artifex', the alchemist or craftsman</term> of this art must be most excellently learned in the sciences of <term>natural philosophy: the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science</term>, and must possess a naturally deep <term>intellect: 'ingenium', referring to innate talent, wit, or creative power</term>, along with a great and fervent desire for this craftsmanship. For that which is not acquired through natural talent is assisted by art and instruction. It is necessary, therefore, for the practitioner to be supported by the most profound investigation.

For no matter how much knowledge he may have gained through instruction, unless he is aided by natural <term>diligence: 'industria', a combination of hard work and practical cleverness</term>, he will not be invited to our most precious feasts <note>original: 'epulas tam preciossimas'; this is a metaphorical reference to the successful completion of the Great Work, which was often described as a divine or royal banquet for the enlightened</note>. Indeed, through his own diligence, he will be able to correct an error in an instant, whereas he would not know how to apply a remedy if he were grounded in theory alone; for art is aided by intellect, and intellect by art.

Furthermore, it is necessary for the practitioner himself to be of a constant will in his work, lest he presume to try now this thing and now that; for in a multitude of things our art <note>The text breaks here; the catchword 'stra' indicates the next word on the following page is 'nostra', meaning 'our'</note>...

<summary>This chapter defines the ideal alchemist as a blend of theoretical knowledge and innate talent, arguing that practical ingenuity (industria) is essential for correcting the inevitable errors that arise during the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>natural philosophy, ingenium, art and theory, diligence, alchemist's character, constancy, practitioner</keywords>

...is not perfected in our art. *For there is one <term>stone: 'lapis', the Philosopher's Stone</term>; one <term>medicine: 'medicina', the curative agent or elixir</term>; one <term>decoction: 'decoctio', the process of heating or boiling</term>; in which the whole <term>mastery: 'magisterium', the complete alchemical process</term> consists*, to which we add no foreign thing nor do we take anything away, except that in its preparation, we remove the superfluous. <note>The author emphasizes the "unity" of the work—a core alchemical belief that the transformative power comes from a single source material that only needs to be purified of "superfluous" impurities, not mixed with external additives.</note>

For it is necessary for the practitioner to insist diligently on the work until its completion, lest he leave the work already begun cut short <note>original: 'detruncatum', meaning mutilated or lopped off</note>, because he would acquire neither knowledge nor progress from a cut-short and diminished work, but rather loss and despair. It is also expedient that he not be ignorant of the principles and primary roots of this art, which belong to the essence of the work, because he who is ignorant of the beginning will not find the end.

We, moreover, shall tell you, dearest son <note>In alchemical literature, the "son" refers to the "Son of Art"—the dedicated student or initiate to whom the master's secrets are revealed</note>, in this book all those principles in complete discourse, and also sufficiently open and manifest to the wise, according to every fitting requirement of the art. It is also expedient that the practitioner himself be mild and tempered against anger, lest perhaps suddenly, because of an impulse of anger, he scatter and destroy the work already begun. <note>Practical advice for the laboratory: alchemical processes often took months of delicate heating; a moment of frustration could lead to breaking fragile glass vessels or overturning a furnace.</note> Similarly ex-

...because the substance of those principles is uniform. Therefore, if such a <term>breakdown: *resolutio*, the process of a substance dissolving into its component parts</term> were to occur suddenly, it would certainly happen to both of them together. Since the <term>moist: *humidum*</term> does not differ from the <term>dry: *siccum*</term> at that stage, because of their powerful mixing, the moist would certainly be broken down along with the dry. For this reason, the whole would vanish into smoke, and the moist could not be separated from the dry during the breakdown because of the strong union they have with one another.

We see a clear proof of this in the <term>sublimation: *sublimatio*, the alchemical process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then collects again as a solid</term> of <term>spirits: *spirituum*, volatile substances like mercury or sulfur that can "fly" from the fire</term>. Instead, their whole substance rises up together, or very little of their component parts is dissolved.

Therefore, a long-lasting and steady breakdown of this kind of subtle, smoky material is the cause of the <term>thickening: *inspissatio*</term> of metals. ...admit <note>original: *confitemur*; the first part of the word appeared on the previous page</note> that our art should not be yielded. For such critics strengthen their own fantasy and error by arguing from a similarity, or from the "greater" to the "less" <note>A logical fallacy where they assume that because a "greater" thing (creating life) is impossible, a "lesser" thing (refining metals) must also be impossible</note>. Their arguments contain no logical necessity, but are merely contingencies in most cases.

We also show, by another way of demonstration, that they provide no apparent similarity between the **weak mixture** <term>mixture: *mixtio*; the elemental combination of a substance</term> of the composition of animals and the firm and strong composition of minerals. And this is so because in animals and other living beings, where the composition is weak, it is not merely the proportion that completes them, nor the ingredients of the proportion, nor the quality of the ingredients, nor the mixing that results from their mutual action and passion <note>original: *actione... & passione*; refers to the Aristotelian concept of elements acting upon one another and being acted upon</note>—which arises from the gathering of those primary qualities. 

Rather, it is the **soul** <term>soul: *anima*; the life-force or animating principle</term> which, according to the opinion of many, comes from the secrets of nature, such as from the **quintessence** <term>quintessence: *essentia quinta*; the "fifth essence" or celestial element that exists beyond the four earthly elements</term> or from the **First Mover** <note>original: *primò motore*; the ultimate source of all motion and life in the universe</note>. We say this also according to the opinion of many, and we remain ignorant of the secret of this soul. Therefore, although there is a weak mixture in these living things, we do not know how to "complete" <note>In this context, to "complete" means to successfully create or bring to perfection</note> them, because that completing principle, which is the soul—

<meta>Catchword: ani-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that alchemy is possible because metals are purely physical mixtures of elements, whereas living creatures require a divine "soul" or "quintessence" that humans cannot replicate.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy vs biology, soul, quintessence, First Mover, elemental mixture, primary qualities, philosophical necessity</keywords>

soul <note>original: *animam*; the first syllable appeared on the previous page</note>, we do not know how to infuse. By this it is certainly clear that it is not a failure of the **mixture** <term>mixture: *mixtio*; the specific ratio of elements in a substance</term> that prevents us from **perfecting** <note>In this context, "perfecting" an animal means successfully creating or completing its life cycle</note> an ox or a goat; rather, it is from a lack of the infusion of a **soul** <term>soul: *anima*; the vital breath or animating principle of life</term>. For we would indeed know how to create a weak or even weaker composition, or a strong and even stronger one, by imitating the natural way and course through our art. 

In metals, therefore, the "perfection" is of a lesser degree than in living beings, and their perfection revolves more around **proportion** and **composition** than around anything else. Therefore, since the perfection required for metals is less than in the other things we have described, we can more freely perfect these [metals], whereas we cannot do so for the others [animals]. 

Therefore, God, the most high and glorious, varies these perfections among one another in many forms. For in those things where the **composition**—which is according to nature—was weak, He placed a greater and more noble perfection, which is according to the soul. But He established other things of a stronger and firmer composition, such as **stones** and **minerals**; yet in these He placed a lesser

If they argue otherwise, however, claiming that one **species** <term>species: a distinct kind or category of being in the natural hierarchy</term> does not change into another **species**, we say they lie once more—as they are often accustomed to do—for a species does indeed change into another species in this manner: that an individual of one kind is manifestly changed into the species of another. 

For we see a **worm** <note>In this period, "worm" (*vermis*) was a broad term for larvae, maggots, or any small creeping thing</note> change into a **fly** both naturally and through human skill; a strangled calf into **bees** <note>This refers to the ancient belief in "bugonia," where bees were thought to be spontaneously generated from the carcasses of cattle</note>; wheat into **darnel** <note>original: *lolium*; a weed that mimics wheat, which many believed was a degenerate form of the grain</note>; and a strangled dog into worms through the boiling heat of **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction: *putrefactio*; the process of decay, which alchemists viewed as a vital stage of breakdown before a new form could arise</term>. 

But we do not do this ourselves; rather, **nature** does it, which we merely assist. In this same way, we do not [personally] transmute metals, but nature does, which we serve through our ingenious art,

We prepare the **matter** <term>matter: *materia*; the physical substance that receives a new form or nature</term> and we arrange the path, since nature herself always works by herself, while we are truly her assistants <note>original: *administratores*; meaning stewards or servants who provide the necessary conditions for nature to act</note>.

As for the fact that we cannot imitate nature in her principles, we have already determined this from the preceding brief negative discourse, and we shall demonstrate it with a more complete discourse in the following sections. <header>PART TWO</header>

...for they draw conclusions from premises that have no relation to the result they infer. Therefore, this argument of theirs holds as much weight as the saying, "A donkey runs, therefore you are a goat." <note>original: "Asinus currit, ergo tu es capra." This was a common medieval example used to illustrate a non sequitur—a logical fallacy where the conclusion does not follow from the premise.</note> And this is the reason: even if the preparation does not happen in an instant, it does not follow that the form or perfection cannot be granted to the prepared matter in an instant. For preparation is not the perfection itself, but the making-ready <term>making-ready: *habilitatio*; the process of rendering matter capable of receiving a new "form" or essence</term> for receiving that form.

If they also say that it is easier to destroy natural things than to put them together, yet according to them we cannot even destroy gold: then, they argue, we would be even less able to create it. We respond to them in this way: their argument does not lead to a necessary conclusion that would force us to believe gold cannot be made. For while it is destroyed with difficulty and constructed with even greater difficulty, it is not impossible for it to be constructed. We assign the cause of this difficulty to the fact that gold possessed a strong composition, and therefore required a more difficult dissolution <term>dissolution: *resolutio*; the chemical process of breaking a substance down into its primary components</term>; for that reason, it is difficult...

In alchemical literature, the "son" is the student or initiate being guided by the "father" or master.</note>, we have sufficiently presented the fantasies of the sophists <term>sophists: *sophistarum*; here referring to critics who use clever but logically flawed or superficial arguments to deny the truth of alchemy</term> to you.

It remains, therefore—now that we have secured your attention by our promise—to proceed along the path of that promise and define those things which are the principles of nature’s intention. We must discuss the essence of these principles more thoroughly in the sections that follow. After that determination, we will also speak about those things which are the principles of our mastery <term>mastery: *magisterium*; the technical skill, process, and secret knowledge required to complete the alchemical "Great Work"</term>. In the first teaching, however, we will speak individually about each of these principles, while in the following section, we will speak of them universally. But for the present, let us first bring forward the arguments of those who deny our art based on the facts given, along with the refutations of those very arguments.

<header>SECOND PART.</header>

*Division of the opinions of those who suppose the Art to exist.*

## CHAPTER XII.

Supposing this Art <note>original: "artem"; referring to the alchemical art of transmutation.</note> to exist, we find that those who pursue it are multiple and diverse in their intentions. Some indeed seek it in spirits <term>spirits: *spiritibus*; in alchemy, these are volatile substances that can be vaporized by fire, such as mercury, sulfur, arsenic, and sal ammoniac</term>, but others in bodies <term>bodies: *corporibus*; referring to the solid metals, specifically the seven planetary metals</term>. Others seek it in salts and alums, in glass and boraxes <term>boraxes: *bauracijs*; likely referring to various fluxing agents or sodium borate used in metallurgy</term>. Still others affirm that this Art is to be found in all vegetable things. Of all these people, some handed down this divine mastery <term>mastery: *magisterium*; the technical process or "Great Work" of alchemy</term> to their successors by positing it partly well and partly badly, while others posited it entirely badly.

It was out of their many errors that we happened to gather the truth, though only with difficulty and labor, through long conjecture, tedious experience <term>experience: *experientia*; in this context, it implies experimental practice or trial and error</term>, and the investment of great expense. Indeed, their error very often fought against the disposition and reason of our own mind, and brought us to the point of despair. Therefore, let them be cursed <note>original: "Blasphemati sint"; literally "let them be blasphemed," here used as a strong condemnation of those who mislead students</note> forever, because they left behind blasphemies to their successors arising from their own errors—they who

### C H A P T E R XIII.

Therefore, those who assumed that this art is found in sulfur <note>original: "sulphure"; in the "Mercury-Sulfur theory," sulfur was often seen as the active, "masculine" principle of metals.</note> spent their labor on sulfur; yet being ignorant of the perfection of its preparation, they left the mastery <term>mastery: *magisterium*; the finished alchemical product or the technical process of the "Great Work."</term> incomplete. For they believed that cleansing and purification alone within it would be the fulfillment of perfection. Therefore, because they saw this failure in the completion of the work—and it had been settled in their hearts for a very long time that this science could be found in sulfur alone—and since they did not find it there, they argue it cannot be found in anything else. Thus, since it is found neither in this nor in anything else through this single preparation, they argue it can be found nowhere, and that the art does not exist.

<header>PART TWO</header>

Responding to them briefly, we say that they understand very little of the truth in this mastery <term>mastery: *magisterio*; referring to the "Great Work" of alchemy or the highest level of technical skill in the craft.</term>. This is because they suppose that common sulfur <term>common sulfur: *sulphur vulgi*; ordinary brimstone, which the author argues is not the true secret ingredient of the philosophers.</term> is our only matter <term>our matter: *materia nostra*; the secret starting substance required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</term>; and even if this supposition were true, they are still deceived in the method of its preparation, for they believed that sublimation <term>sublimation: *sublimationem*; the process of purifying a substance by heating it into vapor and then cooling it back into a solid.</term> alone would be sufficient.

They are truly like children, shut up in a house from the moment of their birth until old age, who do not imagine that the breadth of the world extends beyond the width of their own house, or beyond what can be perceived by the eye. For these men have not applied effort to many stones <note>original: "lapidibus"; in this context, "stones" refers to various minerals and ores tested by alchemists.</note> and other substances. They have withdrawn their hands from many labors and tedious experiments.

Therefore, they have not been able to perceive from what our matter may be drawn, and from what it may not. Furthermore, because they excused their hands from a wealth of labor, they have rightfully remained ignorant of which labors lead to perfection, and which do not. But regarding why their work was flawed, we say it is because they left behind the burning quality <term>burning quality: *urentiam*; the harsh, corrosive, or combustible nature of crude sulfur that must be "fixed" or removed.</term> in the sulfur, and its flight <note>original: "fugam"; a term for volatility, or the tendency of a substance to evaporate and escape the vessel when heated.</note>, which not only does not

### *Arguments of those who deny the Art as it pertains to Arsenic.*

## C H A P T E R XV.

But others, thinking that this medicine <term>medicine: *medicina*; in alchemy, this refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone capable of "healing" base metals into gold.</term> is found in the same substance and its companion <note>The "companion" refers to arsenic, which was often paired with sulfur in alchemical theory.</note>, and observing more deeply, have not only purified the burning sulfurous nature <term>burning sulfurous nature: *adurentem sulphureitatem*; the combustible component of a mineral that alchemists believed caused it to be consumed by fire rather than transformed.</term> through sublimation; but they also attempted to remove the earthiness <term>earthiness: *terrestreitatem*; the solid, impure, or "gross" parts of a substance that remain after the spirit has been extracted.</term>, though the volatility <note>original: *fuga*; literally "flight," referring to the tendency of a substance to evaporate or escape as vapor when heated.</note> still remained within it.

And these men, just like the first group, argued against the science, attempting to destroy it. 

Responding to them just as we did to the first, we affirm the art: for we know it, we have seen it, and we have touched the truth of it with our own hands.

Certain others looked more deeply into the substance itself; they cleansed it and removed its volatility <note>original: *fugam*; literally "flight," referring to the tendency of a substance to evaporate or escape as vapor when heated.</note> and combustibility <note>original: *adustionem*; the quality of being easily burned or charred, which alchemists sought to remove so the material could withstand the furnace.</note>. As a result, the substance became "fixed" <term>fixed: *fixum*; a state where a substance remains stable in the fire and no longer evaporates.</term> for them, but also earthy and "dead" <note>In alchemy, a "dead" substance has lost its "spirit" or its ability to react and transform other materials.</note>, yielding no proper fusion, but only a glass-like state <term>glass-like state: *vitrificatoriam*; the process of vitrification, where a mineral turns into a brittle, glassy slag rather than a molten metal.</term>. ## CHAPTER XVII.

Now there are others focused on practical experiments who wished to fix <term>fix: *figere*; the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when heated.</term> spirits <term>spirits: *spiritus*; volatile substances like mercury or sulfur that "flee" the fire.</term> into bodies <term>bodies: *corpora*; the solid metals used as a base.</term> without any other preceding preparation of those bodies. But from these attempts, a similar failure <note>original: *delusio*; literally "delusion," referring here to a deceptive or failed experimental result.</note> brought them fear, anguish, and despair; consequently, they were forced to despise this science and argue against it.

The cause of their confusion and misguided belief is this: because during the melting <note>original: *fusione*; the process of liquefying metals through heat.</note> of bodies by fire, the spirits leave those bodies and do not adhere to them firmly. Indeed, they flee the harshness of the fire, while only the material bodies remain behind in it. This is because the spirits cannot endure the pressure of the fire’s ruthlessness <note>original: *impietatis*; literally "impiety," used here to describe the destructive, unyielding nature of intense heat.</note> on account of their tendency to flee <note>original: *fugam*; volatility.</note>, which has not been removed from them.

A similar failure also occurs sometimes because the bodies themselves flee the fire along with the spirits. The reason for this is that when...

And although they might appear to be fixed when they are enclosed within bodies under a weak fire, they are nonetheless not truly fixed. This is because, when the fire's intensity <note>original: *expreſſione*; literally the "pressing out" or manifestation of the fire's power.</note> is applied, they either flee from the bodies, leaving the bodies behind, or they withdraw from them while the bodies remain, or both flee together.

## C H A P T E R  X X I I.

## *Arguments of those who deny the Art, believing it to be found in glass and gems.*

### *C H A P T E R  X X I I I.*

Those who placed the Art <note>original: "ipsam"; referring back to the alchemical secret or the power of transformation.</note> in glass and gemstones believed they could find ways to achieve the alteration of bodies <term>bodies: in alchemical texts, "bodies" (corpora) refers to the solid metals, such as lead, tin, or copper, which the practitioner seeks to transform.</term> through the use of glass and gems. These people were similarly deluded in their work, because a substance does not alter what it cannot enter <note>original: "ingreditur"; meaning to penetrate or permeate the internal structure of the metal.</note>. It is well known that glass and gems do not "enter" metals, because they do not melt with a true fusion <term>fusion: the process of melting materials together so they combine into a single, uniform substance.</term>; therefore, the metallic bodies are not changed by them.

Even if they have attempted to unite glass with a metal—though this is hardly possible—they still do not achieve their intended purpose, because they are merely making glass out of the metallic bodies. Because of this error, they judge their failure to apply to the whole Art, and thus they argue that the Art does not exist. To all of these people, we respond that they are working with unsuitable material. Therefore, because they reach an improper conclusion, they cannot achieve their goal; nor can they abolish this Art simply because of their own errors.

<vocab>glass, gems, fusion, art, bodies, error</vocab>

## Arguments of those who deny the Art, supposing it to be found in salts and alums.
### CHAPTER XXIV.

There are also others who suppose that this Art is found in salts and alums, nitres and boraxes <term>boraxes: naturally occurring mineral salts used as a flux to help metals melt and flow more easily</term>. They can indeed perform experiments with these; however, we believe that the true Art is found in them least of all. Therefore, if after their experiments they find little usefulness in transmutation <note>original: "transmutationis"; the alchemical process of changing one substance into another, specifically base metals into gold.</note>—namely through dissolving, coagulating, roasting <term>roasting: a process of heating a substance to high temperatures in open air to remove impurities</term>, and working in many different ways—it is because the goal is very far removed and the work is extremely laborious. 

There are others, however, who imitate the sayings of the ancient philosophers who affirm that this Art is found in all vegetables <note>original: "vegetabilibus"; in this context, it refers to the entire plant kingdom, not just garden vegetables.</note>. Those who place the cause of the Art in all plant matter suggest something that is indeed possible in theory, but not for them, since they would sooner fail in their life's span than reach their intended goal. Therefore, if such people never find the Art in plants alone, it is not permitted to argue that the Art cannot be found through any labors at all. All those previously mentioned, however, are erring regarding the single matter <note>The text cuts off here as it transitions to the next page, suggesting the author is about to explain the "one true matter" of alchemy.</note>

<sig>E 3</sig>

<vocab>salt, alum, nitre, borax, transmutation, vegetables, philosopher, art</vocab>

<summary>This page critiques alchemists who mistakenly look for the secret of transmutation in mineral salts or plant matter, arguing that while these paths are laborious and often fail, their failure does not prove that alchemy itself is impossible.</summary>

### Concerning the natural principles according to
### the opinion of the ancients.

## CHAPTER XXV.

We therefore suggest to you, following the opinion of certain ancient philosophers who belonged to the school of those practicing this art, that the primary natural principles from which metals <term>metalla: the seven traditional metals—gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, lead, and mercury</term> are procreated are the "stinking spirit" <note>original: "spiritus foetens"; a traditional name for sulfur due to the pungent odor released when it is burned</note>, which is sulfur, and "living water" <note>original: "aqua viua"; typically referring to quicksilver or mercury</note>, which we also allow to be called "dry water" <term>sicca aqua: an alchemical paradox for mercury, which is a liquid that does not wet the hands</term>. Furthermore, we have categorized the stinking spirit: it is white in its hidden <note>occulto: its inner, secret, or potential state</note> aspect and red in its manifest <note>manifesto: its outward, visible appearance</note> aspect. Both, however, are black in the mastery <term>magisterio: the laboratory process or "work"</term> of this

work, it tends toward redness <note>original: "rubedinem"</note>. We shall therefore state in a brief, complete, and clear discourse the nature and generation <note>generationem: the process by which these substances are naturally "born" or created within the earth</note> of each of these principles, and likewise the manner of their generation. It is therefore fitting for us to expand and broaden our discourse, and to provide an individual chapter for each natural principle.

Generally, however, we say that each of these principles is of a very strong composition and uniform substance, as we relate; and this is so because in them the smallest parts of earth are so united with the parts of air, water, and fire, that none of them can abandon the others during dissolution <term>resolutione: the process of breaking a substance down into its component elements</term>. 

Rather, each is dissolved together with the others because of the strong natural union they have with one another. This arises from the extreme subtlety of the smallest parts, which are joined gradually by natural heat that is equally compressed and digested within the bowels of the earth, according to the proper course of nature and the requirements of their essence. And this is according to the opinion of certain ancient philosophers <note>original: "philosophorum"</note>.

### *Concerning natural principles according to the opinion of more recent authors.*
## CHAPTER XXVI.

But other, more recent authors <note>original: "moderniores"; referring to medieval or early Renaissance authorities who followed the "ancients" like Aristotle</note> have argued differently. They claim that those natural principles were not <term>quicksilver: argentum vivum, or mercury</term>, nor <term>sulfur: the "stinking spirit"</term> in their raw states, but were instead altered and transformed into an earthy material. Whence they said that this principle was something other than the "stinking spirit" <note>sulfur</note> and the "fugitive spirit" <note>mercury; so called because it "flees" or evaporates from fire</note> in its natural state. 

And the reason by which they were chiefly moved is this: they did not find in silver mines, or in the mines of other metals, anything that is quicksilver in its raw nature, nor anything that is sulfur. On the contrary, they found each of these substances separately—each in its own proper mine, in its own nature and form. 

And similarly, they affirm this through another logical reason: that there is no transition from one contrary to another except through a mediating middle-ground <note>original: "medium dispensatiuum"; a philosophical principle stating that a substance cannot jump from one extreme to another without a middle state</note>. And for this reason, they are compelled to acknowledge—and rightly so—that there is no direct transition from the softness of quicksilver to the hardness of any metal without a mediating substance.

<summary>This page introduces the "modern" alchemical theory that metals are not made directly from raw mercury and sulfur, but from these substances after they have been transformed into an intermediate "earthy" state. The author supports this by noting that pure mercury and sulfur are rarely found inside metal ore and that a philosophical "middle state" is required for a liquid to become a hard metal.</summary>

<keywords>quicksilver, sulfur, stinking spirit, fugitive spirit, mercury, alchemy, mineralogy, transition of states, medieval philosophy</keywords>

...except through a mediating middle-ground <note>original: "medium dispensatiuum"</note> which exists between the soft and the hard. But in the mines, they do not find anything in which this middle state <note>original: "media dispositio"</note> is preserved. Therefore, for this reason, they are compelled to believe that <term>quicksilver: argentum vivum</term> and sulfur, in their own proper nature, are not the principle intended by nature’s design. On the contrary, [the true principle is] something else which results from their alteration at the root of nature into an earthy substance, in this manner: 

Since each of them is converted into an earthy nature, then from both of these earthy substances a very thin and pure smoke <note>In Aristotelian meteorology, "smoke" or "exhalation" was thought to be the precursor to minerals</note> is released because of the heat compressed in the <term>bowels of the earth: viscera terrae</term>. This "double smoke" is the <term>immediate material: materia immediata</term> or the first principle of metal. 

However, once this smoke has been "cooked" and digested <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the maturation of a substance through steady, moderate heat, mirroring a biological process</note> by the temperate heat of the mine, it is converted into the nature of a certain earth. For this reason, it undergoes a certain <term>fixation: fixio</term> <note>the process by which a vaporous or liquid substance becomes solid and stable against fire</note>. Then, the flowing mineral water running through the bowels of the earth washes over it and dissolves its sponginess; the water becomes uniformly entwined and joined with it in a natural, firm, and true union. And therefore—

All the elements come together in such a <term>mixture: mixtio</term> according to their proper proportions and natural requirements; these two components are mixed together "through their smallest parts" <note>original: "per minima" — a concept in natural philosophy where substances are combined at the most fundamental, microscopic level so they become a single new substance rather than a simple mechanical blend</note> until they create a uniform mixture. 

Through a successive and long-lasting <term>decoction: decoctio</term> <note>the process of "cooking" or heating a substance to concentrate or mature its qualities</note> and <term>digestion: digestio</term> <note>the ripening of a substance through steady, moderate heat, modeled on the biological process of food maturation</note> driven by the <term>natural heat: calor naturalis</term> compressed within the mines of the earth, this mixture is thickened and hardened until nature achieves its goal <note>original: "intentionem" — the purpose or "final cause" toward which a natural process moves</note> and becomes metal. We say, however, that those who have held this opinion are close to the truth, yet they do not grasp the truth in its

### *Division of the natural principles.*
### CHAPTER XXVII.

We have now concluded our general and complete discourse regarding the natural principles of the metals themselves. It remains, therefore, that we now set forth a specific chapter for each individual principle. Since there are three such principles—<term>sulfur: sulphur</term>, <term>arsenic: arsenicum</term>, and <term>quicksilver: argentum vivum</term> <note>literally "living silver," the common name for mercury</note>—we shall first provide a chapter on sulfur, second on arsenic, and third on quicksilver. Following these, we shall provide a specific chapter for each of the metals that are the results of these principles, according to how they are produced by the work of nature. Thereafter, we must move on to those things which are the foundations and operations of this <term>mastery: magisterium</term> <note>a term referring to the alchemical art or the "Great Work" of transforming base metals into noble ones</note>, assigning the specific causes for all these things.

### *Concerning sulfur.*
### CHAPTER XXVIII.

## *On Arsenic.*
### CHAPTER XXIX.

It remains now to conclude for the present regarding <term>Arsenic: Arsenico</term>. We say, moreover, that Arsenic is similarly composed of a subtle matter and is closely related in nature to sulfur. Therefore, it ought not to be defined differently than sulfur. It differs from sulfur, however, in this: that it is a <term>tincture: tinctura</term> <note>a substance capable of deeply coloring or transmuting another</note> of whiteness, whereas sulfur is a tincture of redness <note>The "whiteness" (albedo) and "redness" (rubedo) refer to the two major stages of the alchemical Great Work</note>. There are two types of both sulfur and arsenic, namely the yellow <note>original: citrinum; likely referring to the mineral orpiment</note> and the red <note>original: rubeum; likely referring to the mineral realgar</note>, which are useful for this art; many other types are not. 

Arsenic is <term>fixed: figitur</term> <note>rendered stable and non-volatile when heated</note> just like sulfur, and the <term>sublimation: sublimatio</term> <note>the process of vaporizing a solid and collecting it again as a purified solid</note> of both from the <term>calxes: calcibus</term> of metals <note>the powdery oxides or residues left after a metal has been roasted or "burned"</note> is better, as will be discussed below. However, sulfur and arsenic are not the perfecting materials of this work <note>the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. For they neither complete nor perfect it. They are, however, situational aids. Moreover, that which is bright and flaky should be chosen, and not that which is stony.

<page-num>65</page-num>

# PART THREE.

...is called mercury <note>The text continues from the previous page, completing the word "Mercurius"</note>. In the usage of the ancients, it is a viscous water made from a most subtle, white, sulfurous earth and the clearest water, which has been digested and united by natural heat in the bowels of the earth. This union occurs through the smallest particles and is so strong that the moist is tempered by the dry, and the dry by the moist, until they are created in equal measure.

Therefore, it easily flees from a flat surface because of the liquidity and moisture of its water; yet it does not stick to what touches it, even though it possesses a viscous matter. This is due to the dryness enclosed within it, which tempers the moisture and does not permit it to adhere <note>This accurately describes the high surface tension of mercury, which causes it to form beads rather than "wetting" a surface like water does</note>.

According to some, it is the <term>matter of metals: materia metallorum</term> <note>the fundamental "mother" substance from which all metals are thought to be born</note> when combined with sulfur. It also adheres easily to three minerals, namely: <term>Saturn: Saturno</term> <note>representing lead</note>, <term>Jupiter: Ioui</term> <note>representing tin</note>, and the <term>Sun: Soli</term> <note>representing gold</note>. It adheres to the <term>Moon: Lunae</term> <note>representing silver</note>, however, with more difficulty; and to <term>Venus: Veneri</term> <note>representing copper</note> even more difficultly than to the Moon. To <term>Mars: Marti</term> <note>representing iron</note>, it adheres in no way except through artifice <note>meaning chemical manipulation or specialized techniques</note>. 

From this, you may surely draw out a secret. For it is friendly and compatible with metals, and it is a medium for joining <term>tinctures: tincturas</term> <note>substances capable of transmuting or coloring metals</note>, and nothing is submerged... <note>The text breaks off here; mercury's high density allows almost all materials to float on it, except for gold</note>

### *On the effects of nature, which are metallic bodies.*

## CHAPTER XXXI.

Now indeed, it remains to see the metallic bodies which are the effects of these principles. There are six in number: namely, Gold, Silver, Lead, Tin, Bronze or Copper, and Iron. We say, therefore, that a metal is a mineral body that is <term>fusible: fusibile</term> <note>able to be melted by heat</note> and, under a hammer, extensible in every dimension <note>The classic definition of malleability</note>. Moreover, metal is of a dense substance and most strong composition. Truly, metals have a great affinity for one another...

# Part Three.

...great <note>original: "magnam"</note>: nevertheless, for this reason, the perfect does not perfect the imperfect through their mixing. For if <term>gold: aurum</term> is mixed with <term>lead: plumbum</term> during melting, gold does not come from this lead; rather, it vanishes into the mixture and is burnt away. The gold, however, remains in the <term>examination: examinatione</term> <note>the process of assaying or cupellation used to test the purity of precious metals</note>; and the same must be said regarding the rest of the imperfect bodies. This is the way of the natural course. 

But in our <term>mastery: magisterio</term> <note>the alchemical Magistery or the "Great Work" of transformation</note>, the perfect aids the imperfect: and in our mastery, the imperfect is perfected through itself, without the addition of any outside thing; and by this same mastery, the imperfect is brought to completion. By God, they alter and are altered by one another; they perfect and are perfected by one another, and each is completed by itself alone without any other's help. And this is the <term>perfect white: album perfectum</term> and the <term>perfect red: rubeum perfectum</term> <note>The "White" and "Red" refer to the two key milestones of the alchemical process: the *albedo* (whiteness), which results in the ability to create silver, and the *rubedo* (redness), which represents the final Elixir capable of creating gold</note>.

Now, however, let us set down a specific discourse and description concerning each one individually. And first of **Gold**, and then in order we shall make mention of the others. We say, therefore, that Gold is a metallic body, lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinum," a term used to describe the deep yellow or orange-yellow hue associated with the Sun and Gold</note>, heavy, unified, very shiny, equally <term>digested: digestum</term> <note>in alchemy, "digestion" refers to the slow maturation or "cooking" of substances through steady heat, whether in the earth or a furnace</note> in the belly of the earth and washed for a very long time by mineral water, extendable under the hammer <note>malleable</note> and fusible in fire: enduring the examination of the <term>ash-cupel: cineritij</term> <note>also called cupellation, a process where metals are heated in a porous vessel made of bone ash to separate precious gold or silver from base metals</note> and of <term>cement: cementi</term> <note>a refining process involving corrosive powders and heat to remove impurities from gold</note>.

From these things, you may certainly infer that nothing is called gold unless it possesses the causes of the definition of gold and all its distinguishing characteristics. Whatever, however, colors a metal lemon-yellow from its roots <note>original: "radicitus," meaning at a fundamental or essential level</note> and brings it to equality <note>a state of perfect balance or proportion among its constituent elements</note>, makes gold from every kind of metal. 

And therefore, through the work of nature we consider—and by <term>artifice: artificio</term> <note>the skill or "art" of the alchemist</note>—that <term>copper: æs</term> can be changed into gold. For we have seen in copper mines from which water flowed out, carrying with it very thin scales of copper, and by its long and continuous flow, washing and cleansing them. Then, indeed, when the water ceased to flow, we saw the aforesaid sca-

## *On Saturn or black lead.*

### CHAPTER XXXIV.

**Lead** <note>original: "Plumbum"; referred to by the planetary name Saturn</note> is a metallic body that is very leaden-colored <note>original: "lividum," suggesting a bluish-grey hue</note>, earthy, heavy, and <term>mute: mutum</term> <note>it does not ring or resonate when struck, unlike silver or copper</note>. It possesses little whiteness along with much leaden-grayness; it shuns the <term>ash-cup: cinericium</term> <note>the cupellation process used to refine precious metals</note> and <term>cementation: cementum</term> <note>a purification process using layered powders and heat</note>. It is soft, easily extendable in every dimension with only a small amount of pressure, and is easily melted without reaching a red heat <note>original: "ignitione"</note>.

Moreover, certain fools think and say that **Lead** closely approaches **Gold** in its nature. But because they are stiff-necked <note>original: "duræ cervicis"; a recurring biblical metaphor for the stubborn or arrogant</note>, devoid of all reason, and unable to perceive any truth concerning the most subtle matters—at least as far as they are able—they judge things instead according to the senses. Because they see that lead is heavy and mute, and does not rot <note>corrode or rust</note>, they believe...

And **Jupiter** adheres closely to the **Sun** and **Moon**, and for that reason, it does not easily depart from them in the <term>trial: examine</term> <note>The "examine" refers to the testing of purity in a furnace or cupel</note>. It receives the <term>red tincture: tincturam rubedinis</term>, and there shines in it an inestimable brightness; and in our <term>Mastery: magisterio</term> <note>The "Magisterium" is the alchemical process of transformation</note>, it acquires a perfect weight. It is hardened and cleansed more easily than **Saturn**, as we shall say below. Whoever knows how to remove its vice of "breaking" <note>brittleness</note> will suddenly rejoice in the profit to be gained from it. For it joins with the **Sun** and the **Moon**, and shall never be separated from them.

->**On Venus or Copper.**<-

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

**Venus**, or **Copper** <note>original: "Aes"; while "Aes" can mean bronze or brass, in this alchemical context it refers specifically to the metal associated with the planet Venus: Copper</note>, is a metallic body that is leaden in hue, participating much in a dark redness; it is able to be heated and melted, is extensible under the hammer, and is very resonant, yet it flees from the <term>ash-cup: cineritium</term> <note>The process of cupellation used to refine noble metals</note> and the <term>cement: cementum</term> <note>A refining process where metal plates are layered with chemicals and heated</note>. Therefore, in the depths of its substance, **Venus** portends the color and essence of **Gold**. Truly, it is hammered and heated just as **Silver** and **Gold** are.

For this reason, you should take the secret from her: for she is the medium between the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> and the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, and it happens that her nature is easily converted to either of them; it is a good conversion requiring little labor. She agrees vehemently with <term>Tutty: Tutia</term> <note>Zinc oxide, often found as a crust in smelting furnaces; when combined with copper, it creates brass</note>, which colors her with a good lemon-yellow color; this is done through the <term>Mastery: magisterium</term> <note>The specific alchemical procedure or "Great Work"</note> without which it is not perfected, so that you may draw profit from this. We are certainly excused through her from the labors of her hardening and heating.

Take her, therefore, above the other imperfect metals in the "lesser" and "middle" work: but in the "greater" work, not at all. In this, however, she has a vice inherited from **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, since she turns leaden easily from the infection of vapors and mois- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "moisture" on the next page</note>

->*On Mars or Iron.*<-

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

**Mars**, or **Iron**, is a metallic body that is very leaden in color, yet it participates slightly in redness and an impure whiteness. It is hard, able to be heated in the fire <note>original: "ignibile"; here meaning the metal can be made red-hot or worked in fire without being destroyed</note>, yet not meltable by direct fusion <note>Iron has a high melting point, making it difficult to melt in a standard alchemical furnace compared to lead or tin</note>; it is malleable and very resonant. Moreover, **Mars** is of the most difficult handling, and this is because of its inability to be melted. Yet if it is melted without a <term>medicine: medicina</term> <note>An alchemical agent or catalyst used to transform or "heal" the properties of a metal</note> changing its nature, it is joined to the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> or the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, and is not easily separated from them by any <term>trial: examen</term> <note>A refining or assaying process, such as cupellation</note>.

If, however, it is prepared and joined, it is not separated by any artifice, provided that its nature and <term>fixity: fixio</term> <note>The property of being "fixed," or resistant to change and evaporation when exposed to fire</note> are not changed by it, but only its impurity is removed. It is, therefore, easily a tincture of redness; but a tincture of a good whiteness is achieved with great difficulty. And when it is joined to the **Sun** or **Moon**, it does not change the color of the mixture, but rather increases it in quantity <note>Mars/Iron acts as an "extender" that can increase the volume of precious metals without spoiling their color if properly refined</note>.

->OF BOOK ONE<-

### *The Mastery of the Essence of Metals.*
## CHAPTER XXXVIII.

Among all the imperfect bodies, therefore, **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iupiter"; the alchemical name for **Tin**</note> exists as more splendid, clearer, and of greater perfection; it is more abundantly transmuted into the solar <note>Gold</note> and lunar <note>Silver</note> essence. In it, however, the work is of easy handling but long labor, because of the speed of its <term>liquefaction: liquefactio</term> <note>The process of melting or becoming liquid; tin has a notably low melting point, which the author considers both an advantage and a challenge</note>. 

After it, **Venus** <note>Copper</note> is chosen for a more perfect transformation, though of difficult handling, yet of shorter labor than **Jupiter**. 

**Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, however, after **Venus**, has a diminished perfection in transmutation compared to her; for it is of easy handling, but exists as a work of the longest labor. 

**Mars** <note>Iron</note>, among the other imperfect bodies, is found to be of the least perfection in transmutation; it is found to be of the most difficult handling of all and the longest labor. 

Therefore, whatever bodies are further removed from speed of liquefaction <note>i.e., metals with higher melting points</note> are found to be of more difficult handling in the work of transmutation, such as **Venus** and **Mars**. Those, however, which are more and more...

<summary>This chapter ranks the "imperfect" metals—Tin, Copper, Lead, and Iron—based on their ease of transmutation into Gold and Silver, weighing the difficulty of handling against the time required for the process.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the difficulty of handling and the duration of labor required to transmute imperfect metals like Venus (Copper) and Mars (Iron) compared to more fusible ones.</meta>

->PART THREE<-

...are most and most [affected]. Truly, whichever bodies participate in a greater infection of <term>lividness: lividitas</term> <note>original: "liuiditatis"; refers to a leaden, bluish-gray, or dull color, signifying deep-seated impurities in the metal</note>, these are proven to be of longer labor and lesser perfection. Now, therefore, we have written down in this tradition the natural principles that concern the intention of the nature of the metallic bodies themselves: and similarly, we have truthfully composed the specific discourses of those bodies in individual chapters, according to the opinion of those who were able to see the inner workings of nature, and according to the <term>experiences: experientiae</term> <note>The author emphasizes empirical testing over mere theory</note> through which we arrived at those things by the persistence of a great duration of labor.

## CHAPTER XXXIX.

Now indeed, let us recount those <term>principles: principia</term> <note>Foundational rules or starting points of the alchemical work</note> of this <term>mastery: magisterium</term> which have been determined, and the perfection of the same. Therefore, the principles of this art are the methods of its operations, by which the craftsman applies himself to this mastery; these methods are indeed diverse from one another. For one method is <term>sublimation: sublimatio</term> <note>original: "sublimatio"; the process of vaporizing a solid and condensing it back into a solid form to purify it</note>, and another is <term>descension: descensio</term> <note>original: "descensio"; a method of distillation or melting where the substance is forced downward</note>, and another is <term>distillation: distillatio</term>; another is <term>calcination: calcinatio</term> <note>original: "calcinatio"; heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</note>, another is <term>solution: solutio</term> <note>original: "solutio"; dissolving a solid substance into a liquid state</note>, another is <term>coagulation: coagulatio</term>, and another is <term>fixation: fixio</term> <note>original: "fixio"; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note>; the eighth and last is <term>ceration: ceratio</term> <note>original: "ceratio"; a process of softening a substance until it reaches a wax-like (cera) consistency</note>. We shall provide a specific account of each of these individually in the sections that follow.

Now the <term>perfection: perfectio</term> of this <term>Mastery: Magisterium</term> <note>The successful completion of the "Great Work," usually resulting in the Philosopher's Stone or the transmutation of metals</note> consists of the consideration of those things by which the completion of the work is reached; and of the condition of things that aid perfection; and of the consideration of the thing itself which brings about the final perfection; and of the consideration by which it is known whether the Mastery has reached perfection or not. 

Therefore, the consideration of things through which the completion of the work is achieved is the consideration of the <term>manifest substance: substantia manifesta</term> <note>The external, observable physical matter of the chemicals</note> and the manifest colors, and the weights in each of the bodies that are to be changed, as well as those things which are not to be changed from the root of their nature without any <term>artifice: artificio</term> <note>Manual labor or chemical processing by the alchemist, as opposed to processes that happen in nature without human help</note>: and the consideration of the principles of bodies according to their hidden depth and their manifest state, according to their nature without artifice, and similarly those with artifice. 

For if the bodies and their principles were not known in the depth of their nature and in their manifest appearance, both with artifice and without: and if we did not know what was <term>superfluous: superfluum</term> <note>original: "superfluum"; refers to impurities or excess qualities that must be stripped away during purification</note> in them, and what contributed to their perfection, and what was diminished in them, it would be necessary <note>The sentence concludes on the following page: "it would be necessary for us to labor in vain."</note>
