### *On Examinations.*
## CHAPTER XLI.

The consideration of the matter from which it is known whether the <term>Mastery: magisterium</term> <note>The alchemical process or the successful completion of the Great Work</note> is in perfection or not, is the consideration of the examination by the <term>cupel: cinericij</term> <note>A test using a porous vessel made of bone ash to separate precious metals from base metals</note>; of <term>cement: cementi</term> <note>A refining process where metal is layered with salts and heated</note>, of <term>ignition: ignitionis</term> <note>Testing stability by heating to a glowing red</note>, of exposure to the vapors of <term>sharp substances: acutorum</term> <note>Corrosive or acidic vapors used to test a metal's resistance to corrosion</note>, of <term>extinction: extinctionis</term> <note>Quenching or cooling a hot substance in liquid</note>, of the mixing of <term>burning sulfur: sulphuris adurentis</term> which consumes bodies, of reduction after <term>calcination: calcinationem</term> <note>Heating a substance until it is reduced to a powder or "calx"</note>, and of the easy acceptance of <term>Quicksilver: argenti viui</term> <note>Mercury</note>. It is useful to describe all these things with their causes and with clear experiments, by which you will be able to clearly recognize that our words have not erred. And these experiments will be thoroughly known to you. And first, we will describe the principles or modes of operation of this mastery, beginning with <term>sublimation: sublimatione</term> <note>The process of heating a substance so it turns to vapor and then collects as a solid again, used for purification</note>; then, we will speak according to the order that seems most appropriate.

# BOOK ONE
## On sublimation in general. CHAPTER XLII.

->Continuing, therefore, with our proposed purpose,<-

we say that the reason for the discovery of **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting the condensed solid; used here to purify materials</note> was this: because neither the ancients, nor we ourselves, nor even those who will come after us, have found anything else that may be joined with **bodies** <note>original: "corporibus"; in alchemy, "bodies" refers to the non-volatile metals, like gold, lead, or copper</note> except for **spirits** <note>original: "spiritus"; volatile substances that can evaporate, such as sulfur or mercury</note> alone, or something that contains the nature of both body and spirit within itself. And they saw that these spirits, when cast upon bodies without being prepared, either gave imperfect colors or completely corrupted and blackened the metals.

And this occurred according to the diversity of those spirits. For some of the spirits are **burning** and **blackening**, such as **sulfur**, **arsenic**, and **marcasite** <note>original: "marchasita"; a mineral, often iron pyrite or "fool’s gold," used in alchemical preparations</note>. And these indeed corrupt and defoul the bodies entirely. Others, however, do not burn, but similarly flee from the fire <note>referring to "volatility," or the tendency to evaporate when heated</note>, such as every kind of **tutia** <note>original: "tutiae"; zinc oxide, often found as a crust in smelting furnaces</note> and **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note>, and these indeed give imperfect colors. The reason for all of them, however, is because

### PART FOUR

G 2

...because <note>original: "niam", the completion of "quoniam" from the previous page</note> those substances which burn and blacken do so for a twofold reason. The first is because their **burning unctuosity** <term>unctuositas adustiva; an oily, flammable quality believed to be inherent in minerals like sulfur</term>, which by its nature is easily ignited and consequently causes blackening, has not been removed from them. The second reason is the **earthiness** <note>original: "terreitas"; the solid, impure, or stony components of a substance</note> which likewise was not removed from them. In those spirits, however, where a perfect color is not achieved, the cause is earthiness alone, which creates a leaden or dull hue during the "projection" <note>the act of casting an alchemical agent onto a base metal to transform it</note> of colors.

Therefore, we have devised a way to cleanse these **spirits** <note>volatile alchemical materials such as sulfur, mercury, or arsenic</note> from the unctuosity that is the cause of **burning** <note>original: "adustionis"</note>, and from the **earthy dregs** <note>original: "terrea feculentia"</note> that bring about a dull appearance. And we could achieve this through no other **mastery** <term>magisterium; a specialized alchemical operation or technical process</term> except through **sublimation** <note>the process of heating a substance into vapor to separate it from solid impurities, then collecting it as it cools back into a solid</note> alone. For when fire lifts a substance, it always elevates the finer parts; it leaves behind, therefore, the coarser parts.

From this, it is evident that spirits are cleansed of earthiness through sublimation—a quality that both hindered their **penetration** <note>original: "ingressionem"; the ability of the alchemical agent to enter into and merge with a metal</note> and similarly produced an imperfect color. Indeed, it is clear through visual experience that they have been freed from those impurities by means of sublimation.

->### BOOK ONE<-

For you see that through **sublimation** <note>the process of heating a substance to a vapor and then condensing it back into a solid to purify it</note> they become **more brilliant** and **more pervasive**, and they enter and penetrate the density of bodies <note>the solid base metals</note> more easily; and they do not produce a foul color, as they used to do before their sublimation. 

That the tendency to **burn** <note>original: "adustio"; the scorching quality believed to blacken or "wound" the metal</note> is indeed removed through sublimation is likewise evident from a clear experiment: for **arsenic** <note>likely referring to the mineral arsenic sulfides, realgar or orpiment</note>, which before sublimation was prone <note>original: "impium"; literally 'wicked,' here implying it was unruly or volatile</note> to combustion, does not allow itself to be ignited after its sublimation; instead, it simply retreats from the fire without flaming. And you will find the same thing in **sulfur** (if you test it). 

Therefore, because—as we said before—we have seen adherence and entry into bodies with transformation <note>the ability to bond with and change the color/nature of a metal</note> in no substances other than **spirits** <term>spiritibus; the four volatile substances: sulfur, mercury, arsenic, and sal ammoniac</term>, we were compelled to conclude that it is necessary to prepare them by the cleansing that is accomplished through sublimation. And so this was the necessary reason for its invention. It is therefore expedient for us to recount its entire order and method without any omission.

## On the sublimation of sulfur and arsenic.

### CHAPTER 43.

**Sublimation** <note>the process of heating a solid substance until it vaporizes and then allowing it to solidify again at the top of a vessel to purify it</note> is the elevation of a dry substance by fire, such that it adheres to its vessel. Truly, sublimation is varied according to the diversity of the **spirits** <term>spirits; the four volatile substances of alchemy: sulfur, mercury, arsenic, and sal ammoniac</term> that are to be sublimated. For some are processed with ignition <note>high heat that causes glowing</note>; some indeed with a moderate fire; and some with a slow and gentle fire. 

If, therefore, **Sulfur** and **Arsenic** <note>likely referring to natural arsenic sulfides like orpiment or realgar</note> are to be sublimated, it is necessary for them to be sublimated by a slow and gentle fire. This is because they have very fine parts joined uniformly and very strongly to coarse parts; if they were sublimated through a great intensity of fire, their entire substance would surely ascend without any separation or purification—indeed, it would be blackened and burned. 

Therefore, so that one may separate the **impure earthy substance** <note>the "dross" or non-reactive minerals that contaminate the pure alchemical principle</note>, it is necessary to employ two kinds of techniques: namely, the correct proportion of the fire and the cleansing through mixing with **dregs** <note>original: "fecibus"; the solid residue or additive materials like salt or crushed glass used to "hold down" impurities during heating</note>: because the mixing of the spirits to be sublimated with these dregs, the parts com... <note>the text cuts off mid-word, likely "compressed" or "combined"</note>

### PART FOUR

...[that] most subtle substance is the cause of easy **inflammation** <note>original: "inflammationis"; catching fire</note> and **scorching** <term>adustio; a technical term for the unwanted burning or charring of a substance that ruins its alchemical potential</term>. that, once its unclean **earthiness** <note>original: "terreitate"; the solid, non-reactive impurities</note> has been removed through the proper administration of fire, and once that most subtle and smoky part—which brings about scorching and corruption—has likewise been cast away, there might remain for us that part which exists in **equilibrium** <note>original: "aequalitate"; a stable state where the substance is pure and neither too volatile nor too fixed</note>, and which may achieve a simple **melting** <term>fusio; the state of being liquid and flowable over heat</term> over the fire without any scorching, or without fleeing from the fire through its own inflammation. 

Now, the proof that the most subtle part is the cause of burning is demonstrated by the clearest arguments: for fire converts to its own nature each of those things which is most akin to it. But from every combustible thing, that which is subtle is more akin to fire, and that which is even more subtle is more akin still. Therefore, the most subtle part is the most akin to fire [and thus burns most easily].

But it has this much advantage: that which is **sublimated** <term>sublimatio; the process of vaporizing a solid and collecting the purified deposit</term> without **dregs** <term>feces; the solid residue or base used to hold the material during heating</term>, or without the **calxes** <term>calx; the roasted, powdered form of a metal</term> of **bodies** <term>corpora; in alchemy, this refers to the solid metals</term>, results in a large quantity; whereas that which is with dregs is of a smaller quantity. Furthermore, that which is with the calxes of bodies results in the smallest quantity, but is the easiest and involves the shortest labor. 

That, however, which more readily excuses us from the adoption of the dregs of bodies is every kind of **prepared salt** <term>salis pręparati; purified salts, like common salt or saltpeter, used as a chemical medium</term> and things similar to them in nature. With these, the sublimation results in a large quantity and an easy separation of the substance to be sublimated from the salts and the dregs by means of **dissolving the salts** <note>original: "salium solutionem"; because salt dissolves in water while the sublimated spirit (like sulfur) does not, they are easy to separate</note>, which does not occur with any other materials. 

Moreover, the proportion of dregs is such that the quantity of dregs should equal the quantity of the things to be sublimated. In this, the unrefined **artisan** <term>artifex; the alchemist or practitioner of the chemical arts</term> cannot err. For the mediocre and practiced artisan, however, it is expedient to use half the weight of dregs relative to the things to be sublimated, and this happens to be sufficient; in this, he ought not to err. To the practiced and most expert artisan, however, the smallest part of those...

For in a small quantity, a small fire should be applied for the perfection of the sublimation; in a large quantity, a large fire; and in a greater quantity, the greatest fire. 

But since fire is not a measurable thing, it often happens that one errs in its use: sometimes because the **artisan** <term>artifex; the alchemist or chemical practitioner</term> is not experienced, sometimes due to the variation in the proportion of the dregs, and sometimes from differences in the **furnace** and the application of the firewood, and likewise from the **vessels** and their arrangement within the kiln. Regarding all these things, it is necessary for the artisan to be cautious, attentive, and diligent. 

Nevertheless, we shall provide you with a general rule. As for the intended thickness <note>original: "spissitudo"; referring to the insulation provided by the clay or brick walls</note> of the furnace: if you require a great fire, its thickness should be greater, measured to the span of a full hand <note>original: "extensionis palmae cum pollice suo"; the width of a palm plus the thumb, roughly 8 to 9 inches.</note>. Likewise, a similar proportion should be observed in the choice of wood: for solid wood provides a strong fire, while porous wood provides a weak one. Similarly, dry wood provides a large and quickly-exhausted fire, while green <note>original: "viridia"; unseasoned or fresh</note> wood provides a small fire that lasts a long time.

And of this kind are **talc** <note>original: "Talek"</note>, and the **calx** <term>calx: a powdery substance or oxide produced by roasting a mineral or metal</term> of eggshells and of burnt white marble, and glass ground very finely, and every kind of prepared salt. For it is cleansed by these substances. 

However, it is not cleansed by those things with which it shares a nature <note>original: "conuenit"; suggesting chemical compatibility or shared properties</note>, unless they are perfect **bodies** <term>bodies: in alchemy, this usually refers to the seven primary metals (gold, silver, etc.)</term>; instead, it is corrupted and blackened. This is because all such things possess a combustible **sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitatem"; the oily, flammable principle thought to exist in minerals</note>, which, ascending with the mercury during sublimation, corrupts it. 

And you see this through experience: because if you sublimate it from **tin** <note>original: "Stanno"</note> or from **lead** <note>original: "plumbo"</note>, you will find it stained with blackness after the sublimation. Therefore, its sublimation is better performed using those things with which it does not share a nature. Although sublimation from things compatible with it would be better if they did not possess that sulfurousness, for the reasons mentioned before: because it would unite better with them and be more perfectly cleansed. Therefore, [sublimation] from **talc** is better...

# OF THE FIRST BOOK #

...[sublimation from talc] is a [better] sublimation than from all other things: because talc has little affinity with mercury and has no **sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureïtatem"; the flammable, oily principle in minerals</note>. 

The method for removing its superfluous **wateriness** <term>wateriness: (Latin: "aqueïtas") the volatile, moist component of a substance that alchemists sought to remove to achieve "fixation"</term> is this: when the mercury is mixed with the **calxes** <term>calx: a powder produced by roasting a mineral or metal</term> from which it is to be sublimated, it should be ground and mixed with them by soaking <note>original: "imbibitione"</note> it in vinegar or a similar substance until no trace of the mercury remains visible. 

Afterward, the moisture from the soaking is removed over a very slow fire. As this moisture evaporates, the wateriness of the **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti viui"</note> departs along with it. However, let the fire be so very slow that the entire substance of the quicksilver does not rise up and ascend because of it. 

Through multiple repetitions of this soaking, along with grinding and gentle roasting <note>original: "assatione"</note>, the greater part of its wateriness is destroyed. Any remaining moisture is removed through the repetition of the **sublimation** <term>sublimation: heating a substance into a vapor and cooling it back into a solid to purify it</term>. 

And when you see the substance become extremely white—surpassing snow in its whiteness—and clinging like a "dead" thing to the sections of the **aludel** <term>aludel: a series of pear-shaped pots or tubes used as condensers in the sublimation process</term>, then repeat its sublimation over itself without the dregs <note>original: "fecibus"</note>. This is necessary because a fixed portion of the mercury remains stuck to the dregs and never [ascends] through...

### *On the sublimation of Marcasite.*
## CHAPTER XLVI.

We have now sufficiently delivered the summary of the sublimation of **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti viui"; mercury</note>. Now let us pass to the sublimation of **marcasite** <term>marcasite: in historical alchemy, this term often referred to various metallic ores like iron pyrite (fool's gold) or bismuth</term> itself. We say, therefore, that the sublimation of marcasite is twofold. The first is performed without the **ignition** <note>original: "ignitione"; the application of intense heat or fire</note> of the **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic pot or condenser used to collect the vapors of sublimation</term>. The other is indeed done with ignition; this is because it contains within itself a dual substance: namely, one part pure sulfur that is not **fixed** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it no longer evaporates</term> in its nature, and the other part quicksilver that is fixed and moderately prepared. The first is useful in the same way as sulfur; the second is useful as **mortified** <term>mortified: mercury that has been processed until it loses its liquid, "living" metallic form and becomes a solid powder</term> and moderately prepared mercury.

...may be carried out. And let those holes be in the joint where the furnace meets its wheel. The furnace is indeed designed for **high heat** <note>original: "magnae ignitionis"</note>: its supports should be at a height of two **cubits** <term>cubit: an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches, based on the length of the forearm</term>. In the middle, let there be an **iron wheel** <note>original: "rota ferrea"; here used as a grate to hold fuel while allowing air to pass</note>, perforated with very small and very frequent holes, and firmly attached with **lute** <term>lute: a specialized clay or paste used to seal joints and protect glass from direct flame</term>.

The openings of these holes should be narrower at the top and gradually wider toward the bottom, in the shape of a rounded pyramid; this is so that ash, spent coals, and other waste products can fall through them more freely, and the holes themselves remain continuously open for the intake of air. For the free intake of a large quantity of air through these lower holes is one of the primary causes of **high heat** <note>original: "magnae ignitionis"</note> within the furnace. Therefore, apply yourself to this practice and you shall discover it to be true.

The reason for the length of the vessel is so that a large portion of it may extend beyond the fire and be cooled. This ensures that the vapors raised through the furnace by **sublimation** <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a gas and then collects again as a purified solid on a cooler surface</term> find a place of cooling where they may adhere, rather than finding a way to escape or meet their **destru-** <note>The text ends mid-word; "exterminatio" likely continues on the next page, referring to the "destruction" or loss of the volatile spirits.</note>

Consequently, in all things to be sublimated, the vessel should be extended for the greater part of its length into a place for its own cooling. Truly, the reason for the **glazing** <note>original: "vitrificationis"; the application of a glass-like coating to seal the ceramic</note> of the vessel is so that the ascending vapors do not find the wall of the **aludel** to be porous and escape by penetrating through it. Therefore, the place where the vapors are meant to adhere is glazed, so they may find no way of escape. 

The bottom of the **aludel** and the area around it, however, is not glazed. This is because the bottom exists in a state of **high heat** <note>original: "magna ignitione"</note>, which would melt the glazing; once melted, the glazing would cause all the material being sublimated to melt along with it and be turned into glass. For it is the property of glass to conquer all things and convert them into its own nature. With all these...

<header>PART FOUR.</header>

...to be sublimated with great heat. Therefore, all these substances share a single purpose in their sublimation, based on the same principles and the same practical tests.

They follow one general procedure: whatever is meant to be **sublimated** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as a purified solid on a cooler surface</term> by fire must be purified of its **dregs** <term>dregs: or "feces" (Latin: *feces*), the solid impurities or residues left after a process like distillation or sublimation</term>. This is because these materials already contain enough—even an excess of—their own impurities. The proof of this is how difficult it is to sublimate them, even with intense heat.

Moreover, all "diminished" bodies <note>original: *corpora à perfectione diminuta*; this refers to base metals like lead, tin, copper, or iron, which alchemists believed were "imperfect" compared to the "perfection" of gold</note> are sublimated using this same method. No real difference exists, except that the fire required for sublimating these metals must be more intense than the fire used for **magnesia** <term>magnesia: an alchemical term for various substances, often manganese dioxide or magnesium carbonate</term>, **marcasite** <term>marcasite: here likely referring to iron pyrites or similar metallic sulfides</term>, and **tutty** <term>tutty: crude zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term>.

The metals do not differ much in their sublimation processes, either; the only exception is that some require the addition of a "lifting" substance <note>likely referring to the addition of salts like sal ammoniac, which help metals vaporize more easily</note> to help them rise, while others do not. This is done to make their **elevation** <note>original: *eleuatio*; the rising of vapors during the heating process</note> easier.

There is, however, one specific technique for sublimating these metals that has been proven effective through experience: namely, that one should not place a large amount of the material to be sublimated at the bottom of the **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic or glass condenser used to collect the products of sublimation</term>...

<header>BOOK ONE</header>

...since a large quantity hinders **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it vaporizes and then collecting the purified solid as it cools</term>, the bottom of the **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic or glass vessel used in sublimation</term> used for this process must be flat and not concave. This is so that the substance, spread evenly and thinly across the floor of the vessel, can be lifted—that is, vaporized—equally and in great quantity throughout all its parts.

The metallic bodies that require the addition of a "lifting substance" <note>substances added to help metals vaporize more easily, often salts or minerals</note> are **Venus** <note>original: *Venus*; the alchemical name for copper</note> and **Mars** <note>original: *Mars*; the alchemical name for iron</note>, due to the slowness with which they melt. Venus requires **tutty** <term>tutty: crude zinc oxide</term> for this process, while Mars requires **arsenic**. With these additions, they are easily "lifted" into vapor because they align most closely with those substances. Therefore, let their sublimation be carried out after considering these factors, just as with tutty and similar materials. Their sublimation should be organized in the same sequence we described for **marcasite** <term>marcasite: a term used here for metallic sulfides like iron pyrites</term>, following the same principles and practical tests previously mentioned.

->*On Descension. Liquefaction. Fusion.*<-

### CHAPTER XLVIII.

Having thus explained the goals of sublimation along with all its principles and practical applications, it now remains to demonstrate the method of **descension** <term>descension: a method of "downward distillation" where the substance is heated from above so that the liquid or vapor is forced through a filter or tube into a vessel below</term>, along with its causes...

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "descension," a process of downward distillation. The previous page introduced Chapter 48, and this text details the specific reasons why alchemists utilize this method rather than standard heating.</meta>

<header>PART FOUR</header>
<sig>I 2</sig>

...based on the principles and practical tests already mentioned, organized according to its own fixed and complete sequence. There were three reasons for this invention.

The first reason is so that when a substance is enclosed in that vessel known as a **descensorium** <term>descensorium: a specialized vessel used for downward distillation, allowing molten material to flow out of the heat through a hole in the bottom</term> or **Chimia** <note>original: *Chimia*; here referring to the specific alchemical apparatus or vessel</note>, it may flow downward through its opening after it has undergone **fusion** <term>fusion: the process of melting a solid into a liquid state</term>. By this downward flow, we can be certain that the substance has undergone a proper melting.

The second reason is so that "weak bodies" <note>referring to metals with low melting points or those that are easily oxidized, such as lead or tin</note> may be preserved from burning away. This is achieved through that downward flow, following their **reduction** <term>reduction: the chemical process of restoring a metallic "calx" (oxide) back into its solid metallic form</term> from their **calxes** <term>calx: the crumbly, ash-like substance left after a metal has been roasted or "burnt" in the fire; an oxide</term>. When we attempt to reduce these weak bodies from their calxes, we cannot reduce every part of them at the same time, but only a portion at a time. Therefore, if the portion that was first reduced into a metallic body had to wait for the reduction of the whole mass, the greater part of it would vanish or evaporate due to the intensity of the fire. It was therefore necessary to devise a method so that as soon as a part is reduced, it is removed from the heat of the fire. This is made possible by using the *descensorium*.

The third reason for the invention of this method of descension was for the **purification** <note>original: *depu-* (depurationis); the text cuts off mid-word</note> of metallic bodies...

The pellet holds the dregs <note>original: *feces*; the solid impurities or waste left after melting</note> of the bodies just like the descensory does, and even more effectively; therefore, we shall describe its method.

PART FOUR.

<sig>I 4</sig>

...diversity of things to be distilled. For some distillation is done by fire, while some indeed is done without fire. That which is done by fire is found to be of two kinds. One is by elevation into an **alembic** <note>original: *alembicum*; a pear-shaped glass or metal vessel used in distilling</note>; another is by the descent of chemistry, by which means oil is extracted from vegetables. And the general reason for the invention of any distillation is the purification of liquids from the dregs of their own cloudiness <note>original: *turbulentiæ*; refers to the murky or unsettled state of a liquid before it is refined</note> and the preservation of those liquids from rot or corruption. For we see that a thing distilled by whatever kind of distillation is made purer and is better protected against rot. 

However, the specific reason for the invention of that distillation which occurs by ascent in an alembic is the pursuit of pure water from dross, the proof of which is well known: because we see that water distilled twice or thrice through an alembic has no earthy dregs mixed into it. The reason for the invention of pure water was the **imbibition** <term>imbibition: the process of a solid "body" absorbing a liquid "spirit," intended to soften or chemically change the solid</term> of spirits and medicinal powders: so that whenever we require imbibition, we may have pure water; which, after dissolution <note>original: *resolu-* (the sentence continues on the next page); refers to the breaking down of a solid into a liquid state</note>...

Once these are prepared, the fire should be applied until the distillation begins. The alembic and its cucurbit should be made of glass. The fire should be increased according to the needs of the distillation until it is seen that everything intended to be distilled has come over, using a strong application of fire as required by the process.

Then, the distillation vessel, fitted with its alembic as we described before, should be placed upon that layer.

And the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: the gourd-shaped bottom flask containing the substance to be heated</term> should be covered all around with those same grasses or similar materials, up to nearly the neck of the alembic, so that the cucurbit does not break. Over this bedding, thin rods should be scattered, and heavy stones should be placed on top of the rods. By their weight, these stones should press down the cucurbit, the alembic, and the bedding itself, holding them firmly and steadily against the bottom of the pot. This is to prevent them from floating or being tossed about by the water, as such movement could cause the glass vessel to break and the substance being distilled to be lost. Let the narrower part of the filter hang out from the opening of the bowl; under the tip of that filter, place a vessel to receive the distillation. When the filter itself begins to distill, it will first drip the water with which it was moistened. Once that ceases, the liquid to be distilled follows. If this liquid is not clear, let it be poured back into the bowl as many times as necessary until it distills perfectly clear. Because all these things are easy, they [require] great proof...

## *On Calcination.*

### CHAPTER 50.

After the account of distillation, let us turn our discourse to **calcination**. Calcination is, therefore, the reduction of a substance to powder by fire, through the removal of the moisture that holds its parts together. The reason for its invention is so that the corrupting and defiling **sulfureity** <term>sulfureity: in alchemical theory, this refers to the "oily" or combustible sulfur components that were believed to cause metals to tarnish, oxidize, or remain "impure"</term> may be destroyed by fire.

It is diversified, however, according to the diversity of the things to be calcined. Therefore, **bodies** <note>original: *corpora*; in this context, the seven metallic elements</note> are calcined, and **spirits** <note>original: *spiritus*; volatile substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from the fire</note> are calcined, as well as other things foreign to their nature—but with different objectives. Because the **imperfect bodies** <note>These are metals other than Gold and Silver, which were considered "perfect" because they do not oxidize in fire.</note> are of two kinds—namely, the hard, such as **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper</term> and **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for Iron</term>; and the soft, such as **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for Tin</term> and **Saturn** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for Lead</term>—all of which are calcined, it was necessary for them to be calcined with different objectives. Nevertheless, they are calcined with one principal objective: that the corrupting and defiling sulfureity may be removed and destroyed by fire.

<meta>catchword: Here (original: Hic)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces calcination as the alchemical process of using fire to reduce metals and "spirits" to powder. It explains that the primary goal is to purge metals like copper, iron, tin, and lead of their impurities (sulfureity) to prepare them for further transformation.</summary>
<keywords>calcination, sulfureity, copper, iron, tin, lead, purification by fire, alchemical bodies, alchemical spirits</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the theoretical explanation of calcination begun on the previous page, specifically focusing on how the process breaks down the "solidity" of metals to allow fire to reach and remove internal impurities that would otherwise be shielded.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>PART FOUR.</header>

For here is burned away every combustible **sulfureity** <term>sulfureity: in alchemical theory, this refers to the oily or flammable components believed to cause metals to tarnish or "rot" when exposed to fire</term> from whatever thing; which cannot be destroyed without **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature in the air to cause it to crumble into a dry powder or oxide</term>: since the body itself is solid; and because of its solidity and the hiding <gloss>latitation</gloss> of the sulfureity within the continuity of the substance of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury; alchemists believed all metals were composed of Mercury and Sulfur fused together</term>, it is protected by that from being burned. And therefore it was necessary to break the continuity of the bodies and the quicksilver within them, so that the fire, reaching more freely to even the smallest part of it, might be able to burn the sulfureity out of it, and the continuity of the mixed quicksilver might not protect it. 

And the general reason for the invention of the calcination of bodies is the **purification from earthiness** <note>original: *à terreitate depuratio*; the removal of "stony" or "earthy" dross that is not truly metallic</note>. For we find that bodies, through the repetition of calcination and **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of returning a calcined powder back into its solid metallic state</term>, are cleansed and more **fixed** <note>original: *figuntur*; to "fix" a metal means to make it stable and resistant to evaporation or further change when placed in the fire</note>, as we shall say in the following <gloss>sequentibus</gloss> sections. But there is a specific calcination for soft bodies <note>In this context, "soft bodies" refers to Lead (Saturn) and Tin (Jupiter)</note>: so that, along with these two purposes <gloss>intentions</gloss>, there is the goal through it to harden them and make them withstand the fire: which is achieved with the clever repetition of calcination upon them, of which—
->in the follow-<- <note>This catchword points to the start of the next page: "in sequentibus"</note>

The reason for this is that Jupiter has a fugitive substance of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: 'argentum vivum', or mercury; alchemists believed metals were composed of sulfur and mercury</term> enclosed in the depths of its nature. If this substance endures a long stay in a strong fire, it will flee <note>original: *fugiet*; the alchemical term for evaporation or sublimation of volatile components</note>, leaving the metallic body deprived of its "humidity" <note>In alchemy, 'humidity' refers to the internal fluid or 'mercury' that gives metals their ability to melt and remain malleable; without it, the substance becomes brittle and stony</note>. It will then be found more suitable for conversion into **vitrification** than into the **fusion** <term>fusion: the process of melting a solid into a liquid metal</term> of a metallic body. For everything deprived of its own internal humidity yields no fusion except a vitrifying one. 

Therefore, it is necessarily required that the craftsman hasten to reduce it quickly with the swiftest force of fire. Otherwise, it is not reduced. Let him, therefore, practice this and he will know. For because of the great quantity of **earthiness** <note>original: *terreitatis*; in alchemical theory, 'earthiness' was the solid, impure element that made a metal less perfect than gold</note> in them, and the great measure of **combustible and volatile sulfurousness** <note>original: *sulphureitatis adustiuæ & fugientis*; sulfur was believed to be the principle of flammability and color in metals</note>, they are easily brought into the state of **lime** <term>lime: 'calx', a metallic powder produced by roasting metal in a furnace</term> in this way.

And therefore, a **porosity** is caused in them, through which the passing sulfurousness is able to fly away; and the fire, freely approaching it for that reason, is able to burn it and lift it up. 

Through this, therefore, it follows that the parts become rarer <note>i.e., less dense or more porous</note>, and are converted to **ash** through the breakdown of their continuity. This is a manifest experience: for when a plate of **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper</term> is exposed to strong ignition, it will give off a **sulfurous flame**, and will create a **pulverizable scale** <note>This refers to the copper oxide (dross) that flakes off when copper is heated in air</note> upon its surface. This occurs because the combustion of the sulfur must necessarily happen more easily from the parts closer to the surface.

### *On Solution.*

## CHAPTER 51.

Our expanded discourse now touches upon **solution** <term>solution: *solutio*; the process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid state</term>. Solution is the reduction of a solid and dry thing into the essence of water. We say, however, that the perfection of every solution is achieved with waters that are very subtle and extremely sharp, acrid, and bitter, containing no dregs. Such examples include **distilled vinegar** <note>original: *acetum distillatum*; a common alchemical solvent used before the discovery of stronger mineral acids</note>, wild grapes, bitter plums, and pears of great sharpness, as well as pomegranates similarly distilled, and things similar to these. 

The reason for the invention of this process was the **subtilization** <term>subtilization: *subtiliatio*; the process of making a substance more refined, thin, or capable of penetration</term> of those materials which previously offered neither proper melting nor **penetration** <note>original: *ingressionem*; the ability of an alchemical agent to enter and transform the "body" of a metal</note>. Without this process, the great utility of **spirits** <note>original: *spirituum*; volatile substances such as sulfur, mercury, or ammonia that "flee" the fire</note> and those things which share their nature would be lost. For everything that is dissolved must necessarily possess the nature of **salt**, or **alum** <note>original: *aluminis*; a group of hydrated double sulfates, used here to describe the chemical behavior of the substance</note>, or something similar to them. From the works of nature, we can prove that only those things possessing the nature of **salts**, **alums**, and similar substances are **soluble**. <term>salts and alums: *salium & aluminum*; in alchemy, these represent soluble, crystalline minerals that act as bridges between the metallic and non-metallic worlds</term> For considering all its works, we find no others to be dissolved except those. <header>PART FOUR</header>

## On Coagulation.
### CHAPTER 52.

**Coagulation** is the reduction of a liquid thing to a solid substance through the removal of wateriness or the deprivation of moisture. There are therefore two reasons for its discovery. One is the hardening of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti vivi*; mercury</note>; the other is the removal of the wateriness mixed into dissolved medicines. It varies, however, according to the diversity of the things to be coagulated. For in one instance, quicksilver requires coagulation; in another, medicines or other dissolved things do. 

There is, moreover, a twofold coagulation of quicksilver. One is through the deprivation of all the innate moisture within it; the other is through the thickening of that same moisture until it becomes hardened. Nevertheless, congealing it is a very difficult and laborious task that requires great, sharp-witted diligence <note>original: *perspicacitatis industria*; this implies both keen observation and hard work</note>; and this is because of the strong union and composition that it possesses. 

We, however, my son <note>The author uses the traditional address "fili" (son), common in alchemical texts to signify the master passing down wisdom to an apprentice or "son of the art"</note>, will describe for you in this chapter the whole technique of that coagulation. Certain people have therefore thought that the technique of its coagulation would [be...]

Because of this, they were led into a state of stupor and wonder, vehemently claiming that such a goal could not be achieved.

Others, however, assuming by necessity based on **natural principles** <term>natural principles: the fundamental laws of nature as understood by medieval scientists, particularly the idea that heat consumes moisture to create dryness</term> that any liquid is converted into dryness by the heat of fire, imagined that through perseverance and the continued application of its fire, the substance would **coagulate**. By this continued effort, they reached a point where some converted the material into a **white stone**, and others into a **red stone** <note>original: *album* and *rubeum lapidem*; these colors represent the penultimate and final stages of the alchemical "Great Work"</note>. However, this stone possessed no capacity for melting, nor any power of **penetration** <note>original: *ingressio*; the ability of an alchemical reagent to enter and transform the internal structure of a base metal</note>. Since they could not determine the cause of these varying results, they abandoned the work.

Yet others attempted to **coagulate** it using **medicines** <term>medicines: chemical additives or elixirs intended to "heal" or transform base matter into a higher state</term>, but they were deceived by an illusion: either because they failed to achieve coagulation at all, or because the substance was invisibly thinned by their efforts and vanished as smoke, or because their coagulation did not take the proper form...

### PART FOUR.

->**OF THE FIRST BOOK**<-

...of any body. And being ignorant of the cause of this diversity, they despaired.

Others, however, prepared artificial or ingenious **medicines** <term>medicines: alchemical elixirs or tinctures intended to "heal" or transform base metals</term> in order to coagulate it during **projection** <term>projection: the final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the "Stone" is cast upon molten metal to effect transmutation</term>. But their coagulation was not useful, because they converted it into an **imperfect body** <note>original: *imperfectum corpus*; a base or unstable metal rather than pure gold or silver</note>; and because of their own insufficiency, they were likewise unable to perceive the cause of this. It is therefore useful for us to describe these causes, so that the practitioner may achieve the **mastery** <term>mastery: the *magisterium*, or the perfected knowledge and successful completion of the alchemical Work</term> of its coagulation.

As we have already sufficiently explained, **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</note> is of a uniform substance. For this reason, it is not possible to remove its wateriness or thicken it in a short period of time by keeping it in the fire. Therefore, excessive haste was the cause of the first error. Since it is of a **subtle** <note>original: *subtilis*; fine, volatile, or prone to evaporation</note> substance, it retreats from the fire. Thus, an excess of fire is the cause of error for those from whom it flees. It is easily mixed with **sulfur**, **arsenic**, and **marcasite** <term>marcasite: a medieval term referring to various metallic sulfides, such as iron pyrite or bismuth, believed to share a nature with metals</term>, because of its natural affinity with them. Therefore, through these substances, it appears to be coagu- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "tum" indicates the word is "coagulated" (coagulatum).</note>

If that part is removed, it will not melt.

# BOOK ONE

it already possesses subtle matter in its current state. The methods of discovering this **medicine** <term>medicine: the alchemical agent or elixir used to transform or "heal" base metals into noble ones</term> are through **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again, used to purify substances</term>, as we have already sufficiently described. Likewise, the method of its **fixation** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or "flees" when exposed to heat</term> was described in its own chapter. The method for the **coagulation** <term>coagulation: the process of turning a liquid or dissolved substance into a solid mass</term> of dissolved substances is performed using a flask placed in ashes up to its neck, over a moderate fire, until the watery parts evaporate and the substance hardens.

->*On Fixation.*<-
### CHAPTER LIII.

**Fixation** <note>original: *fixio*</note> is the proper adaptation of a substance that would otherwise flee from fire, making it compatible with the fire itself. The reason for its discovery was so that every **tincture** <term>tincture: in alchemy, a substance capable of imbuing another material with its properties or color, such as turning silver to gold</term> and every change wrought by the medicine might be made permanent in the altered material, ensuring it is neither changed nor separated from it by the heat of the fire.

This process is itself varied according to the many types of things to be fixed. These include certain bodies that fall short of perfection—such as **Saturn** <note>original: *Saturnus*; the alchemical name for lead</note>, **Jupiter** <note>original: *Iupiter*; the alchemical name for tin</note>, and **Venus** <note>original: *Venus*; the alchemical name for copper</note>—and the spirits, namely **sulfur** and **arsenic** in one degree, and **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum viuum*; mercury</note> in another; in the th... <meta>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword "tio" suggests the next word is "tertio," meaning "in the third [degree]."</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the coagulation of the alchemical elixir and begins a new chapter on fixation, the process of stabilizing volatile substances against heat. It identifies lead, tin, copper, sulfur, arsenic, and mercury as the primary materials requiring this stabilization.</summary>

<header>->PART FOUR.<-</header>

...tion <note>This completes the word "fixation" from the previous page.</note>, as well as **marcasite**, **magnesia**, and **tutia** <term>tutia: a term for zinc oxide, often collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term>, and other substances of a similar nature. These **bodies** <note>referring to metals</note> that fall short of perfection are fixed through their **calcination** <term>calcination: the reduction of a substance to a powder by heat, typically to remove volatile impurities</term> and **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of restoring a calcined substance or ore back to its metallic state</term>. They are fixed because through this process they are freed from the combustible and volatile **sulfurous quality** <note>original: *sulphureitas*</note> that corrupts them. We have already spoken sufficiently of this in our previous discourse, specifically in the chapter on calcination.

Moreover, **sulfur** and **arsenic** are fixed in two ways. First, by repeating the process of **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process where a solid turns into vapor and then back to solid, used for purification</term> upon them in an **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic vessel used in alchemical sublimations, often stacked in series</term> until they remain stable. The purpose of speeding up this fixation is so that you might devise a way to perform multiple repeated sublimations in a short time. This is done using two aludels with their double covers in the following order or manner: namely, that the act of sublimation never ceases until the substances have been fixed. Therefore, let that which has risen into one cover be immediately thrown into the other aludel, and thus repeat the process by alternating between them. Never let the materials sit idle or remain stuck to the joints <note>original: *spondilibus*; referring to the segments or connecting rims of the aludel stack</note> of the vessel;

# OF THE FIRST BOOK

...cessation, so that they remain in a continuous **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify and "fix" the substance</term> by fire until their rising ceases. For the shorter the time in which you can complete the multiple repetitions of this sublimation, the more quickly and effectively their **fixation** <term>fixation: the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so that it no longer evaporates</term> will occur.

And for this reason, a second method of fixation was invented: which is through the **precipitation** <note>original: *praecipitationem*; here meaning the act of casting the substance back down</note> of the substance being sublimated into the heat of the fire, so that it remains in that heat continually until it is fixed. This is performed in a long glass vessel with a base made of earthenware rather than glass—since glass would shatter into pieces—which is skillfully joined to the vessel with a secure seal. Using an iron or stone spatula, when the substance has adhered to the **shoulders** <note>original: *spondilia*; the internal sides or ridges of the vessel</note> of the vessel, it is to be cast down into the bottom of the heat. This is done continually by turns until the substance is fixed and no longer rises.

Indeed, the method for the fixation of **mercury** <note>original: *argenti vivi*, literally "living silver"</note> is the same as the method for fixing **sulfur** and **arsenic**. They do not differ from one another except in this: that sulfur and arsenic cannot be fixed unless their thinnest flammable parts are first separated from them by an ingenious and subtle artifice of division...

# PART FOUR

...are prepared, through this last method of **fixation** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so that it no longer evaporates</term>. **Mercury** <note>original: *argentum vivum*, literally "living silver"</note>, however, does not require this consideration, because it is neither burned nor inflamed by fire. Therefore, those substances <note>referring to sulfur and arsenic</note> require a more temperate heat than mercury does in this method. They also differ in this: that they are fixed over a longer time than mercury. Likewise, they differ because they rise higher—due to their **thinness** <note>original: *raritatem*; referring to the low density or volatility of their vapors</note>—than mercury does; and therefore they require a longer vessel than mercury for their fixation.

Now, the method for the fixation of **marcasite**, **magnesia**, and **tutia** <note>these are common alchemical minerals: marcasite (iron pyrite), magnesia (likely manganese ore or magnesium carbonate), and tutia (zinc oxide or "zinc flowers")</note> is as follows: when, after their first **sublimation** <term>sublimation: heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again to purify it</term>, we have acquired what we desire from them, we should discard their dregs and repeat the sublimation upon them. This is done by repeatedly returning that which ascended to the top back to that which remained at the bottom, for each of these substances, until they are fixed.

->*On Ceration.*<-

### CHAPTER LIV.

**Ceration** <term>ceration: from the Latin *cera* (wax); the process of adding liquid to a hard substance to soften it and give it a wax-like consistency, making it easier to melt</term> is the process directed toward **liquefaction** <note>original: *liquefactionem*; the process of making a substance flow or melt easily</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Ceration" from Chapter LIV. On the previous page, the author began defining this process as a way to soften hard or non-melting substances to a wax-like consistency.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...is the **softening** <term>softening: original *mollificatio*; the process of making a brittle or hard material pliable</term> of a hard thing or of a juice that cannot be melted. From this, therefore, it is clear; that the reason for this invention was so that a substance—which previously lacked the ability to penetrate and alter a body because it could not be liquefied—might be softened so that it might flow and gain the power of **penetration** <note>original: *ingressionem*; the ability of an alchemical agent to enter into and transform the "body" of a metal</note>.

Some people, however, have thought that this **ceration** <term>ceration: from *cera* (wax); treating a substance until it has a waxy, meltable texture</term> ought to be performed using liquid oils and waters. But that is an error: it is entirely removed from the natural principles of this **Mastery** <term>Mastery: original *magisterij*; a term for the skilled practice of alchemy</term>, and is especially disproven by the manifest works of nature. For we do not see that nature has placed a **humidity** <note>original: *humiditatem*; in this context, it refers to the internal "moisture" or fluid principle that allows metals to be melted and hammered without shattering</note> in metallic bodies themselves that is easily destroyed, which would be necessary for their melting and softening. 

For if nature had placed in them a humidity that was quickly destroyed by the heat of fire, it would necessarily follow that very quickly—namely, once a single **heating** <term>heating: original *ignitio*; literally "igniting" or heating a substance until it glows</term> and **melting** <note>original: *fusio*</note> were completed—the bodies themselves would happen to be totally deprived of all moisture. Therefore, it would follow from this that after being heated once, any body could neither be **hammered** <note>original: *malleari*; referring to the quality of malleability</note> nor melted.

<summary>This page explains the theory of ceration, arguing that it must replicate the permanent "moisture" found in natural metals rather than using temporary liquids like oil or water. This internal moisture is what allows metals to remain malleable and meltable even after intense heating.</summary>

<keywords>ceration, softening, penetration, mastery, metallic bodies, humidity, ignition, melting, malleability</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the process of "ceration" (softening to a waxy state). The previous page ended with the argument that if nature used easily destroyed moisture, metals would lose their ability to melt after a single heating. This page concludes that sentence by stating such a loss of nature would not "be possible."</meta>

->### PART FOUR<-

...be possible. Wherefore, in as much as we are able to imitate the works of nature, it is necessary that we follow nature’s method in **cerating** <term>cerating: original *cerando*; the alchemical process of treating a substance until it reaches a waxy, meltable consistency</term>. Nature herself cerates at the very root of the softening of meltable things by using a **humidity** <note>original: *humiditate*; in alchemical theory, this is the internal "moisture" or fluid principle that allows a metal to melt and flow rather than shatter or turn to ash</note> which, above all other forms of moisture, is able to endure the heat of fire. Therefore, it is necessarily beneficial for us to perform our ceration with a similar humidity.

In no other substances is this cerating humidity found more effectively or more plausibly than in these: namely, in **sulfur** and **arsenic** <note>original: *sulphure & arsenico*; sulfur and arsenic were classified as "spirits" because they could be vaporized by heat and were thought to give metals their color and combustible properties</note> to a close degree. Yet it is found more perfectly and better still in **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: original *argento vivo*; mercury, viewed as the essential "liquid" component of all metallic bodies</term>. For we do not see the humidity of these substances leave behind their **earth** <note>original: *terram*; the solid, dry residue of a substance</note> after **resolution** <term>resolution: original *resolutionem*; the process of breaking a substance down into its parts or melting it</term>, because of the powerful union they achieved during nature's own process of mixing. 

However, in all other things that possess humidity, you will find through experiment that their moisture is separated from their earthy substance during resolution; and after that separation, the substance is deprived of all humidity. In the aforementioned **spirits** <note>original: *spiritibus*; substances that can be sublimated or vaporized</note>, and especially in quicksilver, this happens least of all. Therefore, there is no other thing through which from their...

<summary>This page argues that alchemical ceration must use the "natural humidity" found in sulfur, arsenic, and mercury, as these substances possess a unique moisture that resists being destroyed by fire. The author explains that unlike other liquids, these "spirits" remain bonded to their solid parts even when melted, which is essential for transforming metals.</summary>

### BOOK I. PART FOUR

...[union] of the **ceration** <term>ceration: here *inceratione*; the process of softening a substance to a waxy state</term>; by that acceptance, we may be excused. # THE ALCHEMICAL TRADITION OF HIGHEST PERFECTION BY GEBER THE ARAB:
<note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an eighth-century polymath often considered the father of chemistry. This text represents the "Summa Perfectionis," one of the most influential works in the history of medieval alchemy.</note>
## BOOK TWO.

->*On the consideration of perfection and of those things*<-
->*which pertain to it.*<-

### CHAPTER I.

Now that the discussion regarding the principles of this **mastery** <term>mastery: original *magisterij*; refers to the "Magistery," the complete process or the "Great Work" of alchemical transformation</term> has been handed down; it remains to fulfill the intent of our art with a discourse appropriate to it. The consideration of perfection itself is, in fact, the consideration of the **perfecting medicine** <note>original: *perficientiæ medicinæ*; in alchemy, the "medicine" or "tincture" is the transformative substance (often called the Philosopher's Stone) that "cures" base metals of their "illness" or imperfection to turn them into gold</note>, so that it may be examined from which substance that medicine may be most effectively or most closely extracted for the perfection of every imperfect thing. And likewise, we must consider the **techniques** <term>techniques: original *artificiorum*; the specific laboratory operations and manual skills</term> by which we may recognize whether the perfection is complete. Once these matters have been delivered, the entire knowledge of perfection will have been handed down, according to the requirements of our art.

<summary>This page introduces the second book of Geber's treatise, shifting focus from the basic principles of substances to the theoretical and practical study of "perfection" and the "medicine" that transforms base metals.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Jabir ibn Hayyan, Book Two, mastery, perfecting medicine, alchemical perfection, techniques, transformation</keywords>

## BOOK TWO (Continued)

### *On the knowledge of the principles of bodies.*
### CHAPTER II.

Truly, it is not possible to understand this **transmutation** <term>transmutation: the alchemical process of changing one substance, typically a base metal like lead, into another, like gold</term> of bodies and of **mercury** <note>original: *argentum vivum*, literally "living silver" or quicksilver</note> itself; unless the true knowledge of their nature, according to their **roots** <note>original: *radices*; in medieval science, this refers to the fundamental or "radical" components that make up a substance's essence</note>, enters the mind of the craftsman. Therefore, we will first make known the principles of bodies according to their roots and their essences: specifically, what they are according to all the causes of their differences, and what of good or evil <note>in this context, "good or evil" refers to favorable or unfavorable qualities for the alchemical work</note> they contain within themselves. After this, we shall describe the natures and essences of all these bodies with all their causes and properties; namely, which are the causes of their corruption and perfection, proving them with their manifest experiments.

### *On the knowledge of sulfur and arsenic in particular.*
### CHAPTER III.

Therefore, we first affirm the nature of the **spirits** <term>spirits: original *spirituum*; in alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances that can be easily vaporized or "sublimated" by heat, as opposed to "bodies," which are solid metals</term>, which are the principles of those bodies: namely **sulfur**, **arsenic**, and **mercury**.

<summary>Geber explains that successful transmutation requires a deep understanding of the fundamental "roots" of metals. He identifies sulfur, arsenic, and mercury as the three "spirits" or primary volatile principles that serve as the building blocks for all metallic bodies.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, mercury, sulfur, arsenic, spirits, principles of bodies, Jabir ibn Hayyan, alchemical theory</keywords>

We say, therefore, that **sulfur** and **arsenic** are the **fatness of the earth** <note>original: *pinguedo terræ*; a common medieval term for the oily, combustible principle found within minerals</note>, as was previously recounted by us. You may draw a manifest proof of this through experience by their easy **inflammation** <note>original: *inflammationem*; their ability to catch fire</note> and their easy **liquefaction** <note>original: *liquefactionem*; the process of melting or turning to liquid</note> by fire. For nothing is easily inflamed unless it is oily; nor does a substance melt easily through heat unless it is of that nature. 

Sulfur, therefore, and its companion <note>referring to arsenic, mentioned in the opening sentence</note> contain a cause of **corruption** <note>in alchemy, "corruption" refers to impurities or qualities that prevent a metal from becoming gold or silver</note>: an inflammable substance and earthy dregs. However, they also possess a "perfecting" cause that is intermediate, situated between these two extremes. 

The cause of their corruption is therefore their **earthiness** <term>earthiness: original *terreitas*; the solid, non-volatile, and "dirty" part of a mineral that does not melt or combine well</term>, because they possess neither **fusion** <term>fusion: original *fusionem*; the ability of a substance to melt and flow together with a metal</term> nor **ingression** <term>ingression: original *ingressionem*; the ability of an alchemical reagent to penetrate into the interior of a base metal to transform its essence</term>. Likewise, their inflammable substance is a cause of corruption because it neither "stands" <note>original: *stat*; in alchemy, to "stand" means to remain stable or "fixed" under the heat of fire without evaporating</note> nor makes other things stand; instead, it produces blackness and a leaden, livid color in everything of its kind. 

The cause of perfection in them, therefore, is their moderate substance: because, due to its earthiness, it is not disturbed by the ingression which is perfected through good fusion. And through its...

# BOOK TWO
## On the Nature of Mercury.
### CHAPTER IV.

In the case of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: also known as *Argentum vivum* or Mercury; a liquid metal of central importance in alchemy</term>, it is likewise necessary to cut away what is superfluous. For it possesses two causes of corruption from which it is purged during **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance into vapor so it collects in purified solid form at the top of a vessel</term>; namely, one earthy, impure substance that is non-flammable, and another superfluous, fugitive substance of a watery nature. 

Nevertheless, some have thought that it does not contain any superfluous earth or impurity. But their thinking is in vain. For we see that it has much lividness <note>original: *lividitatis*; a leaden or dull bluish-grey color that suggests the presence of base metals or impurities</note> and is not of a pure whiteness; and likewise, through a simple **artifice** <note>original: *artificium*; a skillful alchemical technique</note>, we see black and dreg-like earth emanate from it—specifically through washing, the method of which we shall now describe. 

But because it is possible to derive a twofold perfection through it—namely, that a **medicine** <note>original: *medicina*; in this context, it refers to the "Philosopher's Stone" or the "Elixir" used to transform metals</note> may be created from it, and that it may be perfected through **coagulation** <term>coagulation: the process of turning a liquid substance into a solid</term>—it is therefore necessary to prepare it through a twofold degree of cleansing. Furthermore, two cleansings of Mercury are necessary. One is by sublimation for the purpose of the medicine, and this...

Similarly, it is necessary for us to provide a special instruction regarding the other **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances, such as sulfur or mercury, that can be vaporized or "lifted" by the heat of a furnace</term>: which are **Marchesite**, **Magnesia**, and **Tutia**, all of which produce a great effect upon the **bodies** <note>original: *corporibus*; in alchemy, "bodies" refers to the solid, non-volatile metals like gold, silver, or copper</note>. Therefore, let us describe what they are in their nature, according to their proofs and causes. 

We say, then, that **Marchesite** <note>original: *Marchesita*; likely refers to pyrite or similar metallic sulfides</note> possesses a dual substance in its nature. One part is **mortified quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum mortificatum*; mercury that has been "killed" or chemically altered so that it is no longer a flowing liquid</note>, which has been moderately prepared and is approaching the nature of a **fixed** <term>fixed: a substance that has been made stable and resistant to evaporation by heat</term> substance. The other part is truly a combustible **sulfur** that is not fixed. 

We have discovered that it possesses this sulfurous quality through clear experience. For when it is **sublimated** <note>original: *sublimatur*; the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collects again as a solid at the top of the vessel</note>, a clear, combustible sulfurous substance is lifted out of it. And its sulfurous nature is perceived similarly even without the use of sublimation. For if it is placed in the fire to be ignited, it does not receive the heat until it is first set aflame by the inflammation of the sulfur, and it burns. 

Yet it is also demonstrated to perceptibly contain the substance of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti vivi*</note>. For it bestows the whiteness of pure silver upon **Venus** <note>original: *Veneri*; the alchemical name for copper, which was symbolically linked to the planet Venus</note>, just as the [quicksilver] itself...

Therefore, whatever things are altered through the power of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti vivi*</note>, or of **sulfur**, or of those things similar to them, must necessarily be altered; since these alone communicate and are united in nature with the bodies themselves.

The **Sun**, therefore, is created from a most subtle substance of quicksilver and a most clear and pure **fixity** <term>fixity: the quality of a substance that allows it to endure the heat of a furnace without evaporating or being destroyed</term>, and from a small substance of pure...

### CHAPTER VIII.

If, however, there should be a **fixed, earthy sulfur** mixed in its nature with a **fixed, earthy quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*</note>, and both of these are not pure but of a leaden whiteness <note>original: *albedinis lividae*; "livid" refers to a dull, bluish-gray or lead-like color</note>; and if the quantity of the sulfur is much greater than the quicksilver, **iron** is generated from them. And because the predominance of fixed sulfur in the mixture prevents melting <note>original: *fusionem*</note>, it follows that the sulfur, through the process of **fixation** <term>fixio: the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to fire</term>, loses its readiness to liquefy more quickly than the quicksilver does.

Indeed, we observe that what is not fixed melts more quickly than quicksilver. From these things, the cause of the speed or slowness of melting in each of the metallic bodies is made manifest. For that which contains more fixed sulfur melts more slowly; however, that which contains more **burning sulfur** <term>adurente: "combustible" or "inflammable" sulfur, which alchemists believed caused metals to be easily consumed or melted by fire</term> undergoes melting more easily and rapidly. This has been shown quite clearly by us. Therefore, that fixed sulfur itself causes a slower melting in metallic bodies is proven by the fact that it never [melts], unless it is calci-

is maintained <note>original: *netur*; this is the conclusion of a word from the previous page, likely *tenetur* (is held/maintained)</note>: it can be fixed, and when it has been **calcined** <term>calcinatio: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder or "ash"</term>, it allows for no **fusion** <note>original: *fusionem*; refers to the ability of a metal to melt into a liquid state</note>. But through successive and repeated sublimation by a tempered fire, it is fixed in a similar way and is not turned into earth; rather, it provides a metallic and good fusion. This, moreover, is clear and proven by those who have experienced both methods of its fixation until its completion: both the hurried precipitation and the slow process through successive sublimations.

...the absence of burning; this is achieved through its lack of combustible sulfurousness. Therefore, by this means, we have made a true discovery of a remarkable nature regarding two secrets: namely, the three-fold cause of the corruption of every metal by fire. 

The first of these is the inclusion of burning **sulfurousness** <note>In alchemical theory, sulfur was considered the "combustible" principle; too much "unclean" sulfur caused a metal to be easily destroyed by fire</note> deep within their substance. Through its ignition, it diminishes the entire substance of these bodies and drives it out as smoke in final consumption, regardless of how much **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; the "passive" or "metallic" principle that provides weight and malleability</note> of good melting quality may have been present in them. 

The second cause is the intensification of an external flame upon them, which penetrates them and dissolves them along with itself into smoke, regardless of the degree of **fixation** <term>fixio: the state of being resistant to evaporation or change by fire</term> they might have possessed. 

The third cause is the **rarefaction** <note>the process of making a substance less dense or more porous, essentially opening its "pores" to the flame</note> of the bodies themselves through **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a metal until it oxidizes and turns into a crumbly powder or "calx"</term>. For then the flame of the fire is able to penetrate into them and drive them out as smoke, no matter how **perfect** <note>in alchemy, "perfect" metals refer to gold and silver, which usually resist corruption</note> they might have been. 

Therefore, if all these causes of corruption coincide, the bodies must necessarily be corrupted to the highest degree. If, however, they do not all coincide, the speed of corruption is lessened in each—

Returning, therefore, to the subject at hand, we say that if the **sulfur** should be impure, coarse, and **fixed** <note>original: *fixum*; in alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to high heat</note> for the most part; and if it is a reddish-livid color, neither exceeding nor being exceeded in its entirety; and if it should fall upon <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</note> that is coarse and unclean, it is necessary that **copper** <note>original: *æs*; this term can refer to copper, bronze, or brass, generally representing the base red/gold metals</note> be created from them. ...you may draw out the secret essence. For you see that every substance, having been changed into earth or ash by the action of heat, is easily dissolved and reduced into the **essence of water**. This happens, moreover, because of the <term>refinement</term> <note>original: *subtiliationem*; the process of making a substance less dense, more pure, or more "subtle" through heat</note> of its parts by the fire. Thus, it is more easily dissolved: and so those things which are most refined are most easily dissolved.

From these facts, the cause of the **corruption** and **staining** <note>original: *corruptionis & infectionis*; in alchemy, "corruption" refers to the degradation or rusting of a metal, while "infection" refers to its chemical alteration or the way it takes on a new color or "stain"</note> of these two bodies—namely **Mars** <note>alchemical name for Iron</note> and **Venus** <note>alchemical name for Copper</note>—is made clear. But the other type is **innate**; it is impossible for it to be removed by any ingenious method of the arts performed through **fire**. Our operation cannot suitably or usefully reach a point where it is removed, because it is already united with the substance at the root of its creation. This is proven by experiment: we see that **combustible sulfurousness** is destroyed by fire, but **fixed sulfurousness** <term>fixed: a substance that is stable in fire and does not evaporate or change state</term> is not at all.

### OF THE SECOND BOOK

...approximating <note>original: *approximanti*; continuing the thought from the previous page regarding lead's internal composition</note> to be uniformly mixed and united with the substance of its **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: the metallic principle of fluidity, also known as mercury</term>. And therefore, this opens a path for us to investigate this work: why in **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of reducing a metal to a powder through intense heat</term> Jupiter is changed more easily into a hard substance than **Saturn** <note>the alchemical name for lead</note>; yet, Jupiter does not change more quickly than Saturn in the slowing of **liquefaction** <note>original: *liquefactionis*; the process of melting or becoming liquid</note>. 

This is because Saturn possesses more of the cause of hardness than Jupiter. For the cause of rapid liquefaction is twofold: namely, quicksilver and **non-fixed sulfur** <term>non-fixed sulfur: sulfur that has not yet been stabilized or "tamed," causing it to evaporate or react quickly when heated</term>. Either one of these is sufficient to achieve perfect melting at every degree with a certain ignition—and even without ignition, in the case of quicksilver.

->P A R T   O N E<-

Because, therefore, in **Jupiter** <note>the alchemical name for tin</note> there is a great quantity of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: the metallic principle of fluidity and malleability, also known as mercury</term> that is not well **fixed** <note>original: *non benè fixi*; referring to a substance that has not yet been rendered stable or resistant to the action of fire</note>, a high speed of **liquefaction** <note>the process of melting or becoming liquid</note> remains within it, and this quality is not easily removed from it. 

The cause of **softness** <note>original: *molliciei*</note> is likewise twofold: namely, non-fixed quicksilver and **combustible sulfur** <term>combustible sulfur: the oily, active principle believed to cause metals to burn or change color under heat</term>. Because, therefore, the combustible superfluity of sulfur is removed from Jupiter more easily than from **Saturn** <note>the alchemical name for lead</note>, it follows that once one of the causes of its softness is removed, it must necessarily harden more quickly. 

**Saturn**, however, has both causes of softness strongly joined together, and for that reason, it does not harden easily. Yet, there is a difference between softness caused by **quicksilver** and softness caused by **combustible sulfur**. This is because softness caused by combustible sulfur is brittle or yielding, whereas softness caused by non-fixed **quicksilver** is **extensible** <note>original: *extensiua*; meaning the metal can be stretched or hammered out—malleability</note>. 

And this is necessarily proven by the fact that we see bodies containing a large quantity of quicksilver possess great extensibility, while bodies with little of it possess little. Therefore, **Jupiter** easi- <note>original: *facilius*; the word is completed on the following page as "easily"</note>

<summary>This page explains that the physical properties of tin (Jupiter) and lead (Saturn) are determined by the balance of two principles: quicksilver, which provides malleability, and sulfur, which contributes to melting and brittleness.</summary>
