<header>BOOK TWO</header>

...more finely and subtly extended than **Saturn** <note>the alchemical name for lead</note>: **Saturn** more easily than **Venus** <note>the alchemical name for copper</note>: **Venus** than **Mars** <note>the alchemical name for iron</note>: **Luna** <note>the alchemical name for silver</note> more finely than **Jupiter** <note>the alchemical name for tin</note>: **Sol** <note>the alchemical name for gold</note> indeed more finely than **Luna**. 

It is clear, therefore, that the cause of **hardening** <note>original: *indurationis*</note> is **fixed quicksilver** <term>fixed quicksilver: mercury that has been chemically stabilized so that it resists evaporation when heated</term>, and **fixed sulfur**. The cause of **softness**, however, is the opposite. 

The cause of **fusion** <term>fusion: the process of melting or liquefying a metal</term> is twofold: namely, **non-fixed sulfur** and **quicksilver** of whatever kind. But non-fixed sulfur is necessarily the cause of fusion **without ignition** <note>original: *sine ignitione*; refers to melting at a relatively low temperature, before the metal begins to glow red with heat</note>. 

And you clearly see the evidence of this principle through the **projection** <term>projection: the alchemical act of casting a powder or substance onto a molten metal to transform its properties</term> of **arsenic** <note>original: *arsenici*; alchemists used arsenic to whiten metals or lower their melting points</note> upon bodies of difficult fusion. For it renders them of easy fusion, and without ignition. 

The cause of easy fusion is likewise **non-fixed quicksilver**. But the cause of fusion **with ignition** <note>original: *cum ignitione*; melting that requires intense heat where the metal becomes incandescent</note> is **fixed quicksilver**. But the cause of the impediment of any fusion is **fixed sulfur**. 

Through this, therefore, you will be able to elicit the greatest **secret** <note>original: *secretū*</note>: Since, as bodies containing the greatest quantity of **quicksilver** are found to be of the highest perfection; more...

Therefore, it is necessary that metals containing a greater quantity of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti viui*; mercury</note>—even if they are diminished from perfection—approach perfection more closely. Likewise, it happens that those metals which possess a high degree of **sulfurousness** <note>original: *sulphureitatis*; the presence of "burning" sulfur, which alchemists believed caused metals to tarnish or decay</note> are subject to the greatest corruption.

For this reason, it is clear from what has already been stated that **Jupiter** <note>the alchemical name for tin</note> most closely approaches the perfect metal, since it partakes more in the perfection of quicksilver. **Saturn** <note>the alchemical name for lead</note> follows next; **Venus** <note>the alchemical name for copper</note> follows even less; and **Mars** <note>the alchemical name for iron</note> the least of all, insofar as perfection depends on these primary causes.

However, the imperfect metals behave differently when treated with the **medicine** <term>medicine: often called the "Elixir" or "Philosopher's Stone," this is the substance intended to "heal" the "sickness" of base metals and transform them into gold or silver</term> that perfects them. This medicine supplies what is lacking, penetrates into the depths of the metal's body, masks and tempers its darkness and impurity, and brings out a bright luster. From this perspective, **Venus** is the most **perfectible** <note>original: *perfectibilis*; most easily transformed or "healed" into a precious metal</note>; **Mars** is less so; **Jupiter** even less; and **Saturn** the least of all.

Therefore, through the truthful investigation of our labors, it has been discovered that because of the diversity of these metals, different medicines must be prepared. For one metal requires a medicine...

### CHAPTER XII.

We have proven, therefore, by my discourses that **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances such as sulfur, mercury, or arsenic that "flee" the fire but can penetrate and change the nature of solid metals</term> are more closely assimilated and united to **bodies** <note>original: *corporibus*; referring to the solid metals</note>, because we see them join with bodies more amicably than anything else in nature. For by this, it has been brought to us through true discovery that these spirits are the true medicine for the alteration of bodies. And therefore, in every way of ingenuity possible, we have applied ourselves; so that through them [the spirits], we may transform each of the imperfect bodies, by a firm and true mutation, into a perfect **lunar** <note>original: *lunare*; silver, associated with the Moon</note> and **solar** <note>original: *solare*; gold, associated with the Sun</note> body.

### CHAPTER XIII.

Based on the discussions we have already presented, it is now apparent what nature leaves as either superfluous or lacking within its own works—specifically in the bodies of the metals—within each of those which are considered imperfect **bodies** <note>original: *corpora*; in alchemy, the seven metals (Gold, Silver, Mercury, Copper, Iron, Tin, Lead) were called "bodies"</note>, at least to a sufficient degree. Regarding their full completion, however, we shall here supply through an adequate discourse those matters which we omitted in the preceding pages.

Since, therefore, it happens that the imperfect bodies are of two distinct classes of mutability: the first are soft and easily melted, such as **Saturn** <note>original: *Saturnus*; the alchemical name for Lead</note> and **Jupiter** <note>original: *Iupiter*; the alchemical name for Tin</note>. The others, however, are hard and not easily melted, or only melt with intense heat, such as **Mars** <note>original: *Mars*; the alchemical name for Iron</note> and **Venus** <note>original: *Venus*; the alchemical name for Copper</note>. Because of this diversity, nature has necessarily taught us, through its own formation of these substances, to apply different preparations according to their specific requirements.

There are, then, two imperfect bodies of a single class to be transformed: namely "black lead," which in this craft is called <term>melan <note>original: μέλαν; the Greek word for "black"</note></term>, meaning "the black one," or Saturn; and the "clattering" white lead, which in this art is named Jupiter,

## CHAPTER XIV.

The preparation of the essence of **Saturn** <note>original: *Saturnus*; the alchemical name for Lead</note> is applied in many ways, and likewise for **Jupiter** <note>original: *Iovis*; the alchemical name for Tin</note>, according to the multiple degrees of their perfection, and how near or far they stand from that state. For there is one kind of corrupting influence that exists within their depths, arising from the innate root of their nature—namely, the "earthiness" of their sulfurous quality and the impurity of the **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti vivi*; Mercury</note> that was essentially mixed into them at the very principles of their creation. 

There is another kind of corruption, however, which arrives after their first mixing and brings about decay. These things of the first kind are a burning sulfurousness, its impurity, and a foul and unclean substance of quicksilver; all of these are what corrupt the substance of Saturn and Jupiter. But of these, one is impossible to remove through any **medicine** <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" refers to a chemical reagent or elixir used to "cure" or transform a metal.</note> of the first order of any human industry, or through any craft perfected by fire. The other kind, however, may be removed with a small amount of assistance. This indeed must be removed...

This impossibility arises because, in the foundations of the specific nature of this class of bodies <note>The "bodies" refers to the metals being discussed, specifically Lead and Tin.</note>, these substances were mixed into a true **essence** <note>original: *essentiam*; the fundamental nature or "soul" of the metal</note> and became part of their very being. Therefore, since it is not possible to remove the true essence of any natural thing while the thing itself remains, it was not possible for those earlier practitioners to destroy these corrupting elements. Because of this, some of the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemical texts, "Philosophers" almost always refers to the established authorities and practitioners of the alchemical art.</note> believed that the **Art** <note>The "Art" refers to the Great Work of alchemy—the transmutation of base metals into gold or silver.</note> could never be achieved through this path.

But we, in our own time, searching for this **science**, have reached a conclusion: that likewise, through no method of preparation or human ingenuity could we brighten these **bodies** <note>original: *corpora*; the physical metals</note> to their full, clear luster without them instead being entirely corrupted, stained, and completely blackened. For this reason, we too were led into a state of shock and, for a long time, hid ourselves under the shadow of despair. 

Returning therefore to our senses <note>original: *cor nostrum*; literally "to our heart," referring to a state of deep internal reflection</note>, and tormenting ourselves with the immense afflictions of meditative thought, we looked back from the perfection of the bod-

<header>PART ONE.</header>

...bodies <note>Continuing from the previous page: *corpora*</note> are diminished, and in the depths of their nature they exist as something foul; nothing of brilliance can be found in them, since, according to their nature, such a thing is not within them. For nothing can be found in a thing that does not exist in it already. Since, therefore, nothing of perfection is found in these metals, it necessarily follows that nothing superfluous <note>In alchemy, "superfluous" elements are those that can be purged or skimmed away. Here, the author argues the corruption is so deep it isn't just "extra" dirt, but a fundamental lack.</note> is left to be found or removed during the separation of their various substances. 

Because of this, we consider and find that there was something diminished <note>or "lacking"</note> within them, which must necessarily be completed by a **Medicine** <term>medicina: an alchemical agent or elixir used to "heal" or transform base metals into gold</term> that is suitable for them, thereby filling up what is lacking in them. That which is diminished in them is a scarcity of **Quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti viui*</note>, and a lack of the proper **thickening** <term>spissatio: the process of condensing or solidifying a substance, often through the evaporation of moisture</term> of that same substance. 

Therefore, the completion of these metals will consist of the multiplication of **Quicksilver**, its proper **thickening**, and its permanent **fixation** <term>fixio: the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating</term>. This is achieved through the **Medicine** created from that very substance. For when this Medicine has been brought into being from **Quicksilver**, through its own power, it hides and covers their **dullness** <note>original: *fuscedinem*; literally a "darkness" or "swarthiness"</note> by cloaking it with the clarity of its own splendor; it brings forth a brightness, and converts the metal into a state of radiance.

<summary>The author argues that base metals lack "perfection" because they are deficient in Quicksilver and proper fixation. He concludes that they cannot be purified by removal of parts, but must be "healed" and transformed by an alchemical Medicine that supplies what is missing.</summary>

By its own purity, it will convert and strengthen them until their perfection is complete. We shall describe this Medicine fully below in a discourse suited to the subject.

It follows, therefore, from the preceding arguments, that the discovery of perfection must necessarily be twofold. The first way is through a medicine that separates the foul substance from the mixture; the second is through a medicine that covers that foulness by cloaking it in the radiance of its own brilliance, perfecting the metal by illuminating it. 

Since, therefore, nothing superfluous is found in the depths of these bodies—but rather something diminished—if it is nonetheless beneficial to remove any superfluity after the first mixture, it is necessary to take away that which has been added superficially to its nature. ->*On the preparation of Saturn and Jupiter.*<-

### CHAPTER XV.

Saturn and Jupiter are therefore prepared by multiple preparations, according to the necessity of their closer approach to greater perfection. And this is done in two ways; namely, by a common and a special method of preparation.

The common method involves multiple stages of approaching perfection. For one stage of approach is brightness and a clean substance. Another is hardness combined with the ignition <note>original: *ignitione*, referring here to the heat required for melting or the glowing state of the metal</note> of its melting. The third is **fixation** <term>fixio: the process of making a substance stable under heat so it does not evaporate or "fly" away</term> through the removal of the **fugitive substance** <term>substantia fugitiua: volatile components, often associated with sulfur or mercury, that evaporate or cause instability in the metal</term>.

Therefore, they are cleansed and made bright in three ways: either through cleansing agents; or by the method of **calcination** <term>calcinatio: the reduction of a metal to a dry powder or oxide by means of intense heat</term> and **reduction** <term>reductio: the chemical process of restoring a calcined powder back into its metallic form</term>; or through **solution** <term>solutio: dissolving a solid substance into a liquid state to separate its components</term>. 

By means of purifying agents, they are cleansed in two ways: either when reduced into **lime** <note>original: *calce*, here referring to the metallic ash or oxide resulting from heating</note>; or in the nature of their bodies. In lime

Specifically, this is done so that **precipitated mercury** <term>argentum vivum praecipitatum: mercury that has been chemically separated as a solid from a solution</term> may be dissolved, and the **calcined body** <term>corpus calcinatum: a metal or mineral reduced to a fine powder by high heat</term>, which is intended to be hardened, is also dissolved.

From this mixture, the calcined body is combined in alternating turns by **grinding**, **soaking** <term>imbibendo: the alchemical process of a solid absorbing a liquid to alter its properties</term>, **calcining**, and **reducing** <term>reducendo: the process of returning a calcined powder back to its metallic state</term>; until the hard substance becomes **fusible** <note>original: "fusibile"; meaning it is capable of being melted</note> upon being heated. The cleaner these ingredients are, the more perfectly they achieve the transformation.

In a similar way, hard bodies are softened using a related technique. Specifically, they are to be joined and **sublimated** <term>sublimatio: heating a substance to vapor and recollecting it as a solid to purify it</term> with **arsenic** as many times as necessary; and after the arsenic has been sublimated, they should be roasted with a heat proportional to their nature. We have described the method for this in our **Book of Furnaces** <note>original: "libro fornacum"; a reference to a specific technical manual on alchemical equipment and heat management</note>. Finally, they should be reduced with the specific application of fire, following the instructions given, until they soften during melting according to the requirements of the metal's hardness. Indeed, all of these transformations belong to the **first order**...

-> *On the purification of Quicksilver.* <-

### CHAPTER XVIII.

Therefore, from what has been said before, it is necessary to fully describe the purification of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: mercury, often called "living silver" by alchemists because of its liquid, mobile state</term>. We say, therefore, that quicksilver is purified in two ways: either through **sublimation** <term>sublimatio: heating a substance until it vapors and then collecting the solid deposit to ensure purity</term>, the method of which we have already provided; or through **washing** <note>original: "lavacrum"</note>, the method of which is as follows:

Pour the quicksilver into a glass or stone dish, and sprinkle a quantity of **vinegar** <note>original: "aceti"</note> over it, enough to cover it. Then, place it over a slow fire and let it heat until it is just warm enough to be handled with the fingers. Next, stir it with your fingers until it is divided into very tiny parts, like a powder; continue stirring until all the vinegar that was poured into it is completely consumed. Then, whatever **earthiness** <note>original: "terreitatis"; referring to the solid dross or impure mineral matter</note> is found within it should be washed with vinegar and discarded. This process should be repeated upon the material as many times...

### *On the medicines of the three orders.*

## CHAPTER XIX.

### *The first presupposition.*

Let us therefore first present a universal discourse on medicines, along with their causes and manifest experiences. We suggest, therefore, that unless every superfluous thing is removed from the imperfect substances—whether by medicine or by the method of preparation—they will not be perfected. Specifically, all superfluous **sulfurousness** <term>sulphureitas: the alchemical principle of combustibility and impurity that prevents a metal from being noble like gold</term> and every unclean earthiness must be stripped from them. This must be done in such a way that these impurities do not separate from the mixture during **melting** <term>fusio: the act of liquefying a substance by heat</term> after the **projection** <term>proiectio: the crucial step of casting the transformative elixir or "Stone" onto a base metal to initiate its change</term> of the medicine that alters them. When you have achieved this, you possess one of the essential markers of perfection.

### *The second presupposition.*

Likewise, unless the medicine also illuminates and alters the substance into a white or lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinum"; the yellow color associated with the final stages of the Great Work leading to gold</note> color according to the intention...

->OF THE SECOND BOOK<-

...[red color] which you seek, which brings forth a lucid clarity through the splendor of its brightness; for diminished bodies <note>original: "corpora diminuta"; these are "imperfect" metals like lead or tin that are seen as lacking the "fullness" or "health" of gold</note> are not completely perfected in their fulfillment by this perfection.

### *The third presupposition.*

Furthermore, unless the medicine specifically brings about a **lunar** or **solar fusion** <term>fusio lunarem/solarem: the melting process that results in the creation of silver (traditionally associated with the Moon) or gold (associated with the Sun)</term>, the substance is not capable of being altered into its final completion. This is because it does not remain stable during the trials <note>original: "iudiciis"; the rigorous chemical tests used to determine the purity of a metal, such as prolonged heating</note>, but instead separates entirely from the mixture and recedes. This point, which we have determined elsewhere at greater length, is demonstrated in the following sections regarding the cause of **cupellation** <term>cineritium: a refining process where a metal is heated in a cupel (a porous bowl made of bone ash); base metals oxidize and are absorbed, leaving pure gold or silver behind</term>. Additionally, unless the medicine <note>original: "medicina"; the alchemical elixir or "Stone" used to trigger the transformation</note> is made permanent through a firm alteration of the **impression** <note>original: "impressionis"; in Aristotelian terms, this is the "form" or character stamped upon the substance</note>, the change itself is invalid; for if the impression does not persist, it simply vanishes.

### *The fourth presupposition.*

Moreover, unless the medicine brings about the proper **weights of perfection**, it does not effect a change under a firm and true completion of nature—one where there is no deception caused by a mistaken belief <note>The author warns that a metal might look like gold, but if it doesn't have the correct weight, the alchemist has only fooled himself</note>. For the **natural weight** <note>original: "pondus naturae"; the specific gravity or density inherent to a noble metal</note> is one of the definitive signs of perfection. It is clear, therefore, that since the characteristics of perfection are five in number...

<meta>catchword: [ne]cesse (meaning "necessary")</meta>

<summary>This page details the requirements for a permanent alchemical transformation, emphasizing that the resulting metal must survive the "trial of fire" (cupellation) and possess the specific natural weight (density) of gold or silver to be considered "perfect."</summary>

<header>PART ONE.</header>

...it is necessary that the medicine of our **mastery** <note>original: "magisterii"; refers to the "Great Work" or the art of alchemy itself</note> should bring forth these distinctions during **projection** <term>proiectio: the final stage of the alchemical process where the "Stone" or powder is thrown upon molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver</term>. From this, it is therefore clear from which things our **matter** <note>original: "materia"; the "materia prima" or starting substance of the alchemical work</note> is elicited. For it is extracted from those things which are most closely joined to [metallic] bodies and which, as agents of change, adhere to them in a friendly way in their very depths. Therefore, while searching among all other things, we have not found by our discovery any other substance that is more "friendly" to the natures of bodies than **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver" or mercury; alchemists believed mercury was the primary component of all metals due to its ability to dissolve them</term>. By focusing our work upon this, we have found it to be the true medicine for things capable of change, achieving a complete and true transformation that is not a little remarkable.

### The fifth presupposition.

It remains, therefore, for us to specifically describe its substance and the differences in the properties of its substance. And since we have found that it does not change without the administration of that nature’s alteration, we have found that it must likewise necessarily be prepared; for it cannot be mixed into the depths [of a metal] without that...

...the mode of preparation. And it is, namely, that its substance should become such that it may be mixed from its core to the core <note>original: "in profundo usque ad profundum"; literally 'from the depth to the depth,' implying a total molecular or spiritual permeation</note> of a body capable of change, without separation forever. 

However, this does not happen unless it is made very **subtle** <term>subtilietur: the process of refining a substance into its finest, most volatile, or most potent state</term> through a specific and certain preparation described in the chapter on **sublimation** <term>sublimatio: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used by alchemists to purify materials</term>. Nor does it provide luster unless the most brilliant substance is elicited from it through its own unique ingenuity and the method of its operation by means of a suitable fire. 

And it does not provide a perfect **fusion** <note>original: "fusionem"; the state of being melted and easily mixed</note> unless caution is used during its fixation, using this method to soften hard things and to harden soft things. It is such, however, that a sufficient amount of its proportioned **humidity** <note>original: "humiditatis"; in alchemy, the "moist" or liquid principle that allows metals to flow and join together</note> is preserved according to the requirement of the fusion that is sought. 

Through this, therefore, it is clear that such a preparation of it should be prepared and administered, by which the most brilliant and purest substance may be created from it. Then, indeed, let it be fixed and let caution be shown to the same; namely, so that it may be worked by the **artisan** <note>original: "artifex"; the alchemist. ### PART ONE

And it is such, namely, that if you seek through this to soften bodies that are hard to melt, a **slow fire** <term>ignis lentus: a gentle, low-intensity heat used for gradual transformations</term> should be applied at the beginning of their creation. For a slow fire is preservative of humidity and perfective of fusion. 

If, however, you seek to harden soft things, an **intense fire** <term>ignis vehemens: a powerful, high-temperature heat</term> should be prepared. For such a fire is consumptive of humidity and disruptive of fusion. And it is indeed advantageous for the craftsman of sound mind to necessarily consider all these rules in every **medicine** <note>original: "medicina"; here referring to the alchemical elixir or "Stone" used to "heal" or transmute base metals</note>. 

And similarly, it is necessary to bring forth many other considerations regarding the change of **weight** <note>original: "pondus"; a critical factor in alchemy, as gold was the densest and heaviest of the known metals</note>, along with their causes and in the proper order. For through this alone can their parts be condensed, since nothing intervenes <note>The author argues that high density (and thus weight) comes from the particles of the substance being so fine and uniform that they can pack together perfectly without any gaps or "voids" between them</note>; and the condensation of parts is the source and

Therefore, this useful discourse on medicines is completed if we describe the differences between all the medicines.

We have indeed hinted that there are necessarily three different classes of **medicines** <note>original: "medicina"; here referring to the different grades or "orders" of the alchemical elixir used to transform metals</note>. One is of the **first order**; another is of the **second**; and another is of the **third**. 

Now, I call a **medicine of the first order** <term>medicina primi ordinis: the most basic alchemical preparation, which causes a temporary or surface change rather than a permanent transformation</term> every preparation of minerals which, when **projected** <note>original: "proiecta"; the act of casting the alchemical powder onto molten metal</note> upon bodies that are lacking in perfection, impresses an alteration upon them. However, this medicine does not bring about a sufficient completion, for it happens that the altered metal is changed and corrupted when the impression of that medicine totally evaporates. An example of this is every **whitening sublimation** <term>sublimatio dealbatiua: a process of purifying a substance through heat until it turns into a white vapor and re-solidifies, intended to give a metal the appearance of silver</term> of **Venus** <note>original: "Veneris"; the alchemical name for copper</note> or **Mars** <note>original: "Martis"; the alchemical name for iron</note> which has not undergone **fixation** <term>fixio: the process of making a substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to heat</term>. ### *On the differences between medicines of the first, second, and third order; and first: On the medicines of the First order.*

Following the promised sequence of the determined order: since there is one medicine for **bodies** <note>original: "corporum"; in this context, the solid metals</note> and another for **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti vivi"; mercury</note>. And among the bodies, there is one of the first order, another of the second, and another of the third; and likewise for the quicksilver. Let us therefore relate the differences of all medicines of the first order, then of the second, and then of the third. We shall speak of the bodies first; and afterward, we shall deliver the account of the medicine for quicksilver with a complete discourse and in the proper order. We say, therefore, that of the first-order medicine for bodies, one kind is for **hard bodies** and another for **soft bodies**. Among the hard bodies, one pertains to **Venus** <note>original: "Veneris"; copper</note>, another to **Mars** <note>original: "Martis"; iron</note>, and another to the **Moon** <note>original: "Lunae"; silver</note>.

# BOOK TWO

...the substance projected <note>the act of "projection" in alchemy involves throwing a medicinal powder or substance onto a molten metal to transform it</note> whitens it. It is a good whitening of the **first order** <note>alchemists categorized their processes into "orders"; the first order usually involved changing the external appearance or "accidents" of a substance, like its color</note>. Alternatively, project **sublimated arsenic** <note>arsenic purified by heating until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note> only over dissolved and reduced **litharge** <note>original: "litargirium"; lead monoxide, a common byproduct of silver refining</note>, and then project all of this over the molten **Copper** <note>original: "Veneris"; the planet Venus was the standard alchemical symbol for copper</note>: for it whitens it with a refined appearance. 

Or let **Silver** <note>original: "Luna"; the Moon was the symbol for silver</note> and **Copper** be mixed together, and over these let every whitening medicine be projected. For **Silver** is more of a friend to arsenic than almost any other metallic body; therefore, it removes the **brittleness** <note>original: "fractionem"; literally "breaking." Arsenic often makes copper brittle, but silver was thought to act as a stabilizing agent</note> from it. Secondly, **Lead** <note>original: "Saturnus"; the planet Saturn was the symbol for lead</note> has this effect; therefore, we mix these substances with them. 

In another way, we melt sublimated arsenic until it forms into solid pieces. Then, we project these pieces, one after another, onto the molten **Copper**. We instruct, indeed, that it be projected in pieces rather than in powder. This is because powder catches fire more easily than a solid piece; thus, it vanishes more easily than a piece and is consumed before it can even touch the ignited metal. 

Moreover, the redness of the metal is removed in another way—namely with **tutty** <note>original: "tutia"; zinc oxide, a substance often found as a crust in the chimneys of smelting furnaces</note>—and it is thus whitened. But because the whitening provided by tutty is not sufficient on its own, therefore so...

### *The Whitening of Iron.*
## CHAPTER XXI.

It remains, therefore, to tell of the **whitening of Iron** <note>original: "Mars"; the planet Mars was the symbol for iron</note> created from medicines proper to it: which are, according to their essence, of the **first order** <note>the "first order" refers to a superficial transformation, primarily changing the external color of the metal rather than its internal nature</note>; according to which it does not have a proper melting point <note>original: "fusionem"; iron was difficult to melt in traditional alchemical furnaces compared to copper or lead</note>. 

Therefore, it is useful for us to whiten it with a medicine that causes it to melt. Thus, every medicine that whitens **Copper** also whitens **Iron** similarly, with the preparation of the same order. But its specific melting agent is **arsenic**, of whatever kind. With whatever it is whitened and melted, it is expedient...

These are indeed all the **medicines of the first order** <note>preparations that produce minor or superficial changes in a metal's properties, often just altering color or weight</note> that we have presented. However, many modes of these can be multiplied, provided the essence of the **pigmentary substances** <note>original: "pigmentalium"; chemical substances used for coloring or "dyeing" metals</note> is preserved within the variety of methods.

Indeed, there is no medicine for **quicksilver** <note>original: "Argenti vivi"; mercury</note> in this first order; since there is no medicine capable of altering it by a single quality, but rather it requires a complete and total transformation. Some seekers have indeed found more medicines, but one of two things must necessarily occur:

Either it is necessary for them to create the medicine from the same substances, or from those possessing the same nature. Or they compose a medicine which is equivalent to that which does not exist through its alteration; and which benefits neither the world nor the parts of the world, until the **mover** <note>original: "motor"; the driving force or "prime mover" that governs natural motion and celestial spheres</note> in the sublime mobile of nature has rested uncorrupted.

The **medicine of the second order** is, therefore, that which completes imperfect things solely by the addition of a unifying quality of perfection. But since there are many causes of corruption in each of the imperfect bodies—namely, in **Saturn** <note>the alchemical name for lead</note>, there is a **volatile sulfureity** <note>original: "sulphureitas"; an unstable, sulfurous quality that makes the metal prone to "burning" or reacting poorly</note> and the "flight" <note>instability or tendency to evaporate</note> of quicksilver; through which corruption necessarily occurs, along with its **earthiness** <note>original: "terreitas"; the presence of crude, non-metallic or stony impurities</note>.

Let a medicine therefore be made which either removes one of these [corruptions] entirely, or adorns it by **palliation** <note>a temporary or surface-level masking of defects</note>, leaving behind only the other causes of imperfection. Because there is something immutable in bodies that is innate in the very root of those bodies, it cannot be removed by a medicine of the second order. ->### PART ONE<-

...removed from the mixture, it is called a **medicine** <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" or elixir is a substance used to "cure" base metals of their defects to transform them into gold or silver</note> not of the second order, but of the third and greater order. And because we have found that the superfluities <note>excess or waste materials</note> of those things that "fly" <note>original: "fugientium"; volatile elements that evaporate or escape when heated</note> are removed through the method of **calcination** <note>the process of heating a substance to a high temperature in air to oxidize it or remove volatile parts</note>, and that non-innate **earthiness** <note>stony or crude impurities not part of the metal's essence</note> is removed through **reduction** <note>the process of returning a metal from its oxide/calcined state back to its metallic form</note>, it was therefore necessary to find a **medicine of the second order**. This medicine indeed palliates <note>masks or disguises defects rather than removing them entirely at the root</note> that which is innate, softens the hard, and hardens the soft. Namely, in both hard and soft bodies, it should perfectly establish a true **lunar** <note>silver-like; from "lunificum"</note> state from imperfect bodies, according to a completion that is not **sophistical** <note>deceptive or counterfeit</note> but **solar** <note>gold-making; from "solificum"</note> or lunar.

Since it is clear, therefore, that in those bodies which are soft, the haste of their **liquefaction** <note>the speed at which they melt, which can be a defect if they melt too easily</note> cannot be removed by the clever devices of this art’s operations, nor can the impurity innate in the very root of their principles be taken away, it necessarily follows that we must seek out a medicine which, upon **projection** <note>the final stage where the alchemical agent is cast onto the molten base metal</note>, thickens their thinness. By thickening them, it hardens them enough to withstand **liquefaction** and **ignition** <note>the process of being heated until glowing or burning</note>. Conversely, in hard bodies, it should thin their density, and by thinning them, bring them to a sufficient speed of **fusion** <note>melting and flowing</note> along with the proper quality of ignition.

### *Concerning the medicine of the third order.*
### CHAPTER XXV.

The medicine of this order is twofold: namely, **solar** <note>related to gold-making</note> and **lunar** <note>related to silver-making</note>. Yet, in their essence, they are one, and their manner of acting is similar. For this reason, it is called a "single medicine" by our ancestors whose writings we have thoroughly read. However, there is an addition of a **yellowing color** <note>original: "citrinantis coloris"; referring to citrination, a stage in the Great Work where the substance turns yellow/gold</note> which is brought to perfection by the purest substance of **fixed sulfur** <note>sulfur that has been rendered non-volatile so it can withstand the heat of the fire</note>. This is the difference between this one and that—that is, the lunar and the solar: the solar contains this [yellowing color] within itself, while the lunar does not.

Nevertheless, this third order is called the order of the **Great Work** <note>original: "maioris operis"; the Magnum Opus, the ultimate goal of alchemy</note>; and it is called so because it requires the industry of greater wisdom in its administration and in the preparation of its perfection, and a longer labor to reach the completion of truth. Therefore, the medicine of this order does not differ in any way from the essence of the second order, except through the most subtle steps of preparation in its creation, and through a more enduring persistence of labor. We shall describe all these steps when...

->*Concerning the trials for the perfection of the work.*<-
->CHAPTER XXVII.<-

Therefore, omitting the obvious trials of which we have not made a narrative—since they are known to all and certain even without a sharp-witted mind—namely those of weight, color, and **malleability** <note>original: "extenſionis per malleum"; literally "extension by the hammer," the test to see if a metal cracks or spreads when struck</note>, let us instead test the trials of the craft with caution. We must see whether the **projection** <note>original: "proiectio"; the final stage of the work where a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone or elixir is "projected" onto molten base metal to transform it</note> of this art's administration brings about a fulfillment consistent with the truth. These tests are, namely: **cupellation** <note>original: "cineritium"; a refining process using a cupel (a dish made of bone ash) to separate precious metals from base metals like lead</note>, **cementation** <note>original: "cementum"; a dry refining process where metal plates are layered with mineral "cements" and heated to purify them</note>, **ignition** <note>original: "ignitio"; testing how a metal reacts to or survives intense fire</note>, **fusion** <note>original: "fuſio"; melting the metal to observe its purity and behavior</note>, and exposure over the vapors of **corrosives** <note>original: "acutorum"; literally "sharp things," referring to acidic or reactive vapors like vinegar or mineral acids used to test for surface reactions</note>.

exposition, the test by the mixing of **burning sulfur** <note>original: "adurentis fulphuris"; sulfur was used to test purity because it reacts with base metals to form sulfides but does not affect gold</note>, **quenching** <note>original: "extinctio"; the rapid cooling of a heated metal in liquid to test its temper or purity</note>, the repetition of **calcination** <note>original: "calcinationis"; heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a powder</note> and **reduction** <note>original: "reductionis"; the process of returning a calcined powder back into a solid metal</note>, and the easy or difficult acceptance of **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti viui"; mercury, used to see how readily a metal forms an amalgam</note>. ->*Concerning Cupellation.*<-
### CHAPTER XXVIII.

We speak now of **cupellation** <note>original: "cineritio"; a refining process where metals are heated in a shallow, porous bowl called a cupel—often made of bone ash—to separate noble metals like gold and silver from base metals</note> along with all its most evident causes and the manner of its **fixation** <note>original: "fixionis"; the state in which a substance becomes stable and resistant to evaporation or change when exposed to fire</note>. It is, therefore, only the **solar and lunar substance** <note>original: "solaris & lunaris subftantia"; traditional alchemical terms for gold (Sun) and silver (Moon)</note> that endures through the trial of cupellation. In investigating the true differences in the substance of these perfect bodies, and likewise the causes of cupellation, we shall inquire why some metals endure more and others less in the trial of this **mastery** <note>original: "magifterij"; the "Great Work" or the highest level of alchemical skill</note> when they are diminished from their perfection. However, the secret of these two bodies—gold and silver—has already been sufficiently described by us in the depths of their substance. And it is

### *On heating to a glow.*
## CHAPTER 30.

It remains, therefore, for us to deliver the chapter on ignition <note>original: "ignitione"; in this context, it refers to the process of heating a metal until it glows, a crucial diagnostic step in alchemy and metallurgy</note>. We say, then, that bodies of the greatest perfection <note>referring to Gold and Silver</note> are found to reach a determined state of ignition; they receive the fire before they undergo fusion <note>melting</note>. Therefore, we say that if we endeavor to discover their complete alteration, it is necessary to bring the treated bodies to the point of fusion. That is to say, before these bodies of perfection melt, they should sustain ignition with a flaming glow and the beauty of a celestial color <note>original: "cœlestini coloris"; likely referring to the bright, shimmering blue or brilliant light produced by extremely hot, pure metal</note>: this occurs before their heating reaches that whiteness of fire <note>white heat</note> in which the eye can no longer perceive anything. It is clear, then, that their perfect ignition is completed before melting, characterized by an intense redness, and not by that whiteness which the eye cannot behold. For if the bodies being treated are melted before they are heated to a glow, in the complemen-

And if anything is lacking—that is, in weight, color, the beauty of the glow, or similar qualities found through the ingenuity of the preparation—then the artisan’s investigation was not sufficient. Therefore, let him seek again and again, until he finds it among the good things bestowed by divine goodness.

...since the only fusion <note>melting; original: "fusio"</note> of perfection is that which occurs with ignition <term>ignition; original: "ignitione", the process of heating a metal until it glows red-hot without yet melting</term>, yet not with just any kind of heating. Rather, it must be with a glow in which the body <note>the metallic substance</note> does not turn entirely white, and a glow in which no dark discoloration <note>original: "fuscedo"</note> arises from the fire, and in which the body does not suddenly melt and liquefy so that it flows immediately after reaching that glow.

Therefore, when a weak body melts from the slightest application of heat, either without glowing at all or with a dark, dusky glow, the bodies resulting from such a preparation must necessarily be considered imperfect, products of a diminished artifice <note>original: "artificio diminuto"; poor or incomplete craft or technique</note>. 

And if it is not allowed to cool after melting, and its glow is suddenly and completely turned to blackness—and if for this reason it loses its glow before it has hardened—then the body is not at its completion, regardless of what kind of metal it may be. Instead, it is right to judge that such a body belongs to the category of soft, imperfect metals. But if, on the other hand, its glowing before melting is made strong and violent through the laborious application of fire, and re-

### *The fourth part concerning the examination of the perfection of bodies: namely, concerning exposure over the vapors of sharp substances.*

### *Of calcination & reduction.*

## CHAPTER XXXV.

Next, we must investigate the examination of the repetition of **calcination** <term>calcinationis: the process of heating a metal until it is reduced to a powder or 'calx'</term> and **reduction** <term>reductionis: the chemical process of restoring a metal to its solid state from a powder</term>. We have indicated, therefore, that the bodies of perfection <note>referring to gold and silver, which were considered "perfect" because they do not decay</note> are found—through the repetition of **calcination** and **reduction**—to lose nothing of their excellent qualities, whether in color, weight, or quantity (all of which must be watched very closely); nor do they lose their luster, no matter how many times these operations are repeated upon them. Consequently, if through the repetition of the methods of **calcination** and **reduction** from the **calx** <term>calce: the roasted, powdered form of a metal</term>, any of the various types of altered metals lose any part of their quality, one must conclude that the investigator has pursued his inquiry **sophistically** <term>sophisticè: through deceptive or false methods rather than true natural science</term>. Wherefore,

->so that you may know them.<-

## *On the easy reception of Quicksilver.*
### CHAPTER XXXVI.

By now it has been truthfully made clear to you that the bodies of perfection <note>referring to gold and silver</note> are those that contain the greatest quantity and consistency of **quicksilver** <term>argenti viui: literally "living silver," the common alchemical name for mercury</term>. For this reason, quicksilver is most greatly befriended by them. We must therefore conclude that those bodies approach perfection more closely which "drink up" quicksilver in a more friendly manner. The sign of this is the easy **reception** <term>susceptio: the ability of a metal to absorb or bond with mercury, forming an amalgam</term> of quicksilver by the **solar** <note>original: "solari" - referring to gold</note> or **lunar** <note>original: "lunari" - referring to silver</note> body of perfection. Consequently, for this very reason, if an altered body does not easily receive quicksilver into its own substance, it must necessarily be very far from reaching the fullness of perfection.

## *On the completion of the whole work.*
### CHAPTER XXXVII.

Since we have now discussed the known experiences of the sufficiency of the causes of this **mastery** <term>magisterij: the "Magisterium" or the Great Work of alchemy</term>, according to the requirements of our **intended discourse** <note>original: "propositi sermonis"</note>; it remains for us to reach the completion of this entire divine work in one final chapter: and in sum-

...mary, to draw together the mastery <term>magisterium: the Great Work of alchemy</term> that was dispersed throughout the individual chapters into an abbreviated discourse.

We say, therefore: that the summary of the intention of the whole work is nothing other than that the **stone** <note>the Philosophers' Stone, the elusive goal of alchemy</note>, which has been identified in the previous chapters, be taken. Then, indeed, with the diligence of the work, the labor of the **first degree of sublimation** <term>sublimatio: the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor, then collecting the purified solid that reforms</term> should be applied to it. By this process, it is cleansed of corrupting impurity. This is the perfection of sublimation: that the stone be made subtle by it until it reaches the ultimate purity of subtlety, and finally becomes **volatile** <note>original: "volatilis" — meaning it can be easily evaporated by heat</note>.

From this point, indeed, let it be fixed by the methods of **fixation** <term>fixio: the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when exposed to fire</term>, until it may rest stable within the sharpness of the fire. And here the second degree of preparation stands as the goal.

But the stone is similarly managed in a third degree, which consists of the final completion of the preparation; namely, that you make the now fixed stone volatile again by means of sublimation, and the volatile fixed, and the fixed dissolved; and the dissolved once more volatile, and the once more volatile fixed; until it flows and transforms in a certain **gold-making** <note>original: "solifico"</note> and **silver-making** <note>original: "lunifico"</note> completion.

From the repetition, therefore, of this third degree of preparation in the **medicine** <term>medicina: in alchemy, this refers to the Elixir or the Philosophers' Stone, which was thought to "heal" base metals by perfecting them into gold</term>, there results a diversity of multiplication, quality, and transformation. Thus, from these medicines, some may transform seven times their own weight; some indeed ten times; some a hundredfold; some a thousandfold; and some even unto infinity, changing the body into a **gold-making** <note>original: "solificum" — from Sol, the sun, representing gold</note> and true **silver-making** <note>original: "lunificum" — from Luna, the moon, representing silver</note> perfection. From this point, then, let it finally be tested whether the **mastery** <term>magisterium: the completion of the Great Work</term> consists in such perfection.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

But so that we ourselves are not bitten by the envious, we say: that we have not handed down our **science** <note>original: "scientiam" — the secret knowledge of the alchemical art</note> in a continuous narrative, but have scattered it across various chapters. This was done because if it had been handed down in a single sequence, the wicked as well as the virtuous would have seized upon it without distinction. We have likewise hidden it even where we have spoken most openly; not, however, by using riddles, but by addressing the **artisan** <note>the skilled practitioner or "chemist"</note> through a plain series of discourse. And we have written it in this manner of speech just as, by the grace of God alone, the most high and ble- <meta>catchword: nedi- (the word is likely "benedicti," meaning "blessed")</meta>

<header>PART ONE</header>

blessed, sublime, and glorious, it happened to be gathered into our mind—we who wrote it—for He grants and withholds from whom He wills. Therefore, let the **son of the doctrine** <note>original: "doctrinæ filius" — a traditional term for a devoted student or initiate of the alchemical secrets</note> not despair; because if he seeks it, he will find it—not through formal teaching alone, but by the **investigation of nature** <note>original: "indagatione naturæ"</note> through his own inner impulse. For he who seeks **knowledge** <note>original: "scientiam"</note> through the excellence of his own diligence will find it; but he who seeks it only through the inspection of books will arrive at this **most precious art** <term>artem preciosissimam: the Great Work of alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosophers' Stone</term> only with the greatest delay. For we have published this art, investigated by ourselves alone for ourselves alone, and not for others—yet it is most true and entirely certain. However, we have also set forth this art and the path of its investigation to the wise through the methods handed down by us. Through these things we have transmitted, therefore, let the **artisan** <note>original: "artifex" — the practitioner who physically works the materials</note> of a good mind exercise himself; and he will rejoice to have found the gift of God the Most High. Let these words suffice for the **search** <note>original: "perquisitionem"</note> of this matter.

->*The End.*<-

<summary>This concluding page of the treatise emphasizes that true alchemical success comes from a combination of divine grace, diligent personal experimentation, and the observation of nature, rather than mere book-learning. The author warns that while the text is true, it requires a "good mind" and practical effort to unlock.</summary>

<page-num>278</page-num>
<header>ON THE INVESTIGATION</header>

# GEBER THE ARAB
## THE MOST KEEN PHILO-
### *sopher and alchemist's book on the investigation of the Mastery.*

We have handed down to you the investigation of this **noble science** <term>nobilis scientiæ: alchemy, often called the "noble science" because it sought to "ennoble" base metals into gold</term> derived from continuous and frequent diligence in the work, and from intense study of the labor, as well as from the deepest reflections and various related matters. We have released this so that these things may be better and more aptly understood by you, and so that, once they are understood and verified, these and other matters devised and explored regarding them may be more easily and swiftly brought to fulfillment through practical exercise.

And what else is it to investigate the perfection of the art, but to investigate through **reason** <note>original: "per rationem" — emphasizing that alchemy was a rational, proto-scientific pursuit rather than just magic</note>, to attempt, and to test the subtleties and ingenuity of these matters, until—by working, searching, and experimenting—they reach the intended completion? For in this book of ours, whatever we have investigated as previously mentioned, we have written according to the reasoning of our own mind, pertaining—I say—to the art...

<meta>catchword: pertain-</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the preface to a new treatise by Geber, defining alchemical investigation as a rigorous process of rational inquiry, constant physical labor, and experimental testing. It marks the transition from theoretical philosophy to the practical "Mastery" of the alchemical craft.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, Jabir ibn Hayyan, investigation, Mastery, Magisterium, reason, experimental method, alchemical practice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Geber’s treatise on "The Investigation of the Mastery." The author explains the relationship between this introductory text and his more famous work, "The Sum of Perfection," establishing a pedagogical order for the student of alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>279</page-num>
<header>OF THE MASTERY.</header>

...pertaining. Yet let no one think that we have placed this investigation before our book, which is titled **The Sum of Perfection of the Mastery** <note>original: "summa perfectionis magisterii"—this is the most influential alchemical work of the Middle Ages, traditionally attributed to the 8th-century Arab polymath Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), though now believed to be by a 13th-century Latin author</note>. In that book, we have completely concluded—according to the order of our science—those things which we have seen and touched: specifically through experience and certain knowledge. This knowledge we have noted and devised through our scrutiny of the various effects and **transmutations** <term>transmutationes: the alchemical process of changing one substance, usually a base metal like lead, into another, such as gold</term> of natural and mineral things appearing during the actual work.

We have presented our "Sum," which was composed previously, alongside this investigation as a commentary, as if to explain the "Sum" itself. Therefore, by right, this book precedes that one: since through this book of investigations, one finds the science that seeks knowledge of the "perfecting thing." Since, then, this science treats the imperfect bodies of minerals insofar as it grants the power to perfect them, we must first consider two things: namely, **imperfection** and **perfection**. Upon these two we base our intention. Concerning...

<header># ON INVESTIGATION</header>

...things <note>The text begins mid-word, completing the phrase "concerning those things" (rebus) from the previous page</note> that perfect and corrupt, according to what we have investigated through our experience, we have composed this book. This is because opposites, when placed beside one another, reveal their nature more clearly.

The substance which perfects within minerals is the essence of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the historical term for mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" of all metals</term> and **sulfur**, mixed in the correct proportions. Through a long and temperate **decoction** <term>decoctio: a process of slow heating or "cooking" within the earth (or an alchemist's vessel) to mature a substance</term> within the bowels of the clean earth, this substance is thickened and fixed <note>rendered stable and resistant to evaporation or change</note>. This process preserves its **radical moisture** <term>humiditas radicalis: the essential, internal fluid believed to give metals their flexibility and "life," preventing them from becoming dry, brittle, or easily destroyed by fire</term>—a moisture that does not cause decay—and results in a solid substance that is fusible <note>capable of being melted</note> under proper heat and extendable <note>malleable</note> under the hammer.

By defining the nature of this perfecting substance, we can more easily understand the "corrupting thing." This must be understood in the opposite sense: namely, as an impure substance of quicksilver and sulfur mixed without the proper proportions, or cooked either too little or too much in the bowels of an earth that was perhaps unclean. Such a substance is neither properly thickened nor fixed, and it contains moistures that are combustible...

<meta>catchword: les (completing "combustibiles")</meta>

<summary>Geber defines the "perfect" mineral as a balanced, well-matured mixture of mercury and sulfur, characterized by stability and malleability. He establishes the sulfur-mercury theory, explaining that metal corruption results from impure ingredients or improper "cooking" within the earth.</summary>

->**OF THE MASTERY**<-
<note>original: "MAGISTERII" — referring to the Alchemical Magisterium or the "Great Work" of transformation</note>

...and corrupting things <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page describing the nature of impure minerals</note> which are of a thin and porous substance. They either possess fusion <note>the ability to melt</note> without the required ignition <note>proper heat or "fire-readiness"</note>, or they have no fusion at all, and similarly, they cannot endure the hammer <note>they are brittle rather than malleable</note>.

You will find the first definition <note>referring to the "perfecting substance" described on the previous page</note> in these two bodies: namely, in the **Sun** <term>Sol: the alchemical name for Gold</term> and the **Moon** <term>Luna: the alchemical name for Silver</term>, according to the perfection of each.

The second definition <note>referring to the "corrupting" or imperfect nature</note>, however, is found in these four: namely in **Tin**, **Lead**, **Iron**, and **Copper**, according to the imperfection of each.

And because these imperfect bodies cannot be restored to health and perfection unless an opposing process is worked upon them: that is, that which is manifest <note>visible or external</note> is hidden, and that which is hidden is made manifest. This operation and reversal is completed through **preparation**. Therefore, the preparation of the thing must be applied.

To prepare, therefore, is to take away the superfluous, to supply what is absent, and thus to instill true perfection within them. The perfect bodies <note>Gold and Silver</note> do not require this specific preparation. Instead, they require a preparation such that their parts may be made more subtle <note>refined into smaller, more active particles</note>, and be led back from their corporality <note>solid, physical state</note> toward spirituality <note>a volatile or "spirit-like" state capable of being used in further alchemical work</note>—

...ums, vitriols <note>original: "atramentorum" — while literally "inks," in an alchemical context this refers to a group of metallic sulfates or vitriols</note>, and also glass, borax, and those substances of this nature, as well as very sharp vinegar and fire. And because we intend to prepare imperfect bodies with these substances—that is, to cleanse them—if any impurity should then exist in them according to our experience (in which we have become certain), the aforementioned substances must first be cleansed.

### *The Cleansing of Common Salt.*

For common salt is cleansed in this manner. First, let it be burned; once burned, let it be dissolved in common warm water. Once dissolved, let it be distilled <note>The author uses "distilled" here to mean filtered or dripped</note> through a filter, and once distilled, let it be congealed over a slow fire in a glazed dish <term>parapsis: a shallow, glazed earthenware dish or platter</term>. Once congealed, let it be calcined <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures, but below its melting point, to remove impurities or change its physical state</term> for a day and a night in a moderate fire; and keep it, for it is then sufficiently clean.

### *The Cleansing of Salt Alkali.*

Salt alkali <note>Alkali salt was typically derived from the ashes of burnt plants</note> is cleansed in the same way as was said regarding common salt; *it is the blood of glass* <note>original: "Eſt ſanguinis vitri" — a reference to the role of alkali salts as a flux in glassmaking</note>. First, let it be ground; once ground, let it be dissolved in common warm water; afterwards, let it be distilled through a filter; let it be coagulated and cal-

congealed <note>original: "cinetur" — likely a scribal error or variant for *congeletur* (let it be congealed)</note> over a slow fire.

### *The Cleansing of Rock Salt.* <note>original: "salis Gemmae" — refers to halite or transparent, crystalline rock salt</note>

In order for it to be cleansed, let it be treated in all ways as was done with common salt.

### *The Cleansing of Sal Ammoniac.* <note>original: "salis Armoniaci" — ammonium chloride, highly valued in alchemy for its ability to sublimate and its role in dissolving metals</note>

First, let it be ground with an equal proportion of cleansed common salt. Afterwards, let it be sublimated in a high aludel <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic pot used in the process of sublimation</term> until it has been extracted in a totally pure state. Afterwards, let it be dissolved upon a stone <note>original: "porritem" — likely a contraction of *porphyritem* (porphyry stone)</note> in the open air <note>original: "sub diuo" — literally "under the sky"; a method used to allow salts to deliquesce (absorb moisture and turn into liquid)</note>, if you wish to make a "water" <note>an alchemical liquid or solution</note> from it; otherwise, keep it in its sublimated and pure form.

### *The Cleansing of other salts.*

For various kinds of salts are still to be found; these are prepared and cleansed in the manner already described.

### *The Cleansing of Glacial Alum.* <note>original: "aluminis Glaciei" — likely referring to clear, crystalline potassium alum</note>

Many things can be prepared from this without it being cleansed. Some, however, cleanse it in this manner: Let it be placed in an alembic <term>alembic: a distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling vessel and a cooling cap with a delivery tube</term> and let all the humidity be extracted from it, which is of great value in this art. The dregs <note>original: "feces"</note> remaining in the bottom of the vessel should either be dissolved upon a stone, or in the water extracted from them, or be boiled again.

### *The Cleansing of Yemen Alum.* <note>original: "aluminis Iameni" — alum sourced from Yemen was highly valued in the medieval and Renaissance periods for its purity and its role as a mordant in dyeing and chemical operations</note>
Yemen Alum is prepared in the same manner as glacial alum <note>the clear, crystalline alum mentioned on the previous page</note>: except that in this art <note>the alchemical or chemical "art"</note> it is of greater power.

### *The Cleansing of other alums.*
Several other alums are still to be found, which are prepared and cleansed as previously mentioned.

### *The Cleansing of Inks and first concerning the black.* <note>original: "atramentorum" — while "atramentum" literally means "ink," in alchemical texts it specifically refers to "vitriols" or metallic sulfates (like iron sulfate), which were the primary ingredients for making black ink</note>
Black ink is cleansed in this way. First, let it be dissolved with cleansed vinegar: afterwards, let it be distilled through a filter and congealed: and let it be dissolved again with the same liquid that was distilled from it. Then, let the dregs <note>original: "feces" — the solid residue or sediment remaining after a process</note> from this distillation be distilled through an alembic <term>alembic: a distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling vessel and a cap with a long tube to collect condensed vapors</term>: let them be calcined <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to drive off volatile parts or reduce it to a friable powder</term> and afterwards ground, and again dissolved in its own water <note>referring to the liquid previously distilled from the substance</note> and congealed. Or, let the water itself be reserved according to the will of the artisan.

### *The Cleansing of Copperas.* <note>original: "Cuperosae" — copperas or "green vitriol" (ferrous sulfate), used in dyeing and the production of sulfuric acid</note>
Copperas, or vitriol, is cleansed like black ink. However, black ink has a greater earthiness <note>original: "terrestretitatem" — the proportion of solid, impure, or "earthy" matter within the substance</note> than green ink.

### *The Cleansing of the sharpest vinegar.*

Moreover, vinegars of whatever kind they may be, whether sharp or sour, are refined and purified: and their power or effect is improved through distillation <term>distillation: the process of heating a liquid to create vapor and then condensing the vapor back into a liquid to purify it</term>. ...preventing entry and melting. These and such things are superfluities in these aforementioned bodies <note>the "imperfect bodies" or base metals like lead, tin, or copper</note>; which in them, by our experience and certain and ingenious investigation, have been found. And because these superfluities come upon these accidentally <note>as external qualities</note>, and not radically <note>meaning they are not part of the metal's fundamental essence or "root"</note>: the stripping away of these accidents is possible.

And it is necessary, with artificial fire <note>carefully controlled laboratory heat</note> and these aforementioned cleansed substances, to take away the superfluities and remove the accidents, leaving behind the permanent substance of quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury (argentum vivum), viewed by alchemists as the "mother" or passive principle of all metals</term> and radical sulfur <term>radical sulfur: the "father" or active principle of metals, which gives them color and form</term>. And this is the complete preparation of the imperfect bodies and their perfect cleansing, improvement, purification, sublimation <term>sublimation: heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collecting the purified solid that forms when it cools</term>, and refinement of these or of this remaining substance.

And this is done in many ways, according to what the elixir <note>the transforming agent or "philosopher's stone" in its liquid or powder form</note> needs for its preparation. Therefore, this is the general mode of purification. First, all superfluous and corrupting humidity <note>excess moisture that prevents the metal from being stable or "fixed"</note> that ruins the essential parts must be destroyed with proportional fire: and also the subtle and burning superfluity: and this is done by calcining <term>calcining: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to reduce it to a powder or "calx"</term>. Then the whole remaining substance of the bod[ies]—

<header># OF THE MASTERY</header> <note>original: "MAGISTERII"</note>

[...ness] in their calx <term>calx: the crumbly powder or ash that remains after a metal has been roasted or oxidized by fire</term>, namely, the superfluous moisture of burning sulfur and blackness must be gnawed away and destroyed. This is done with the previously mentioned cleansed, sharp, or bitter corrosives <note>likely acidic or alkaline salts used as cleaning agents</note>; until they have become entirely white or red—colored according to the specific nature of the body <note>the metal being treated</note>—clean, pure, and free from all the previously described superfluity or corruption. And this is achieved with these cleansed corrosives through testing, grinding, soaking, and washing.

Afterward, the unclean, foul-smelling, combustible, and coarse earthiness <term>earthiness: the non-metallic, rocky, or "drossy" part of the ore</term> must be totally destroyed or cast aside. This is done with the aforementioned cleansed or pure substances that do not fuse with the metal. These are mixed with the calx, which has been purified in the manner already described, and well-ground without melting or returning the calx to a solid metal state <note>original: "sine fusione seu calcis reductione"</note>. These substances will hold onto the coarse and unclean earthiness, while the "body" of the metal remains pure, cleansed of every corrupting superfluity.

And this process of separation is done by "descending" through the bearded vessel <note>original: "botum barbatum"; a specialized alchemical vessel or furnace used to filter or distill substances downward to separate the pure from the impure</note>. Regarding the general manner of the improvement or refinement <term>refinement: the process of making a substance "subtle" or more spiritually potent</term> of these pure substances...

<summary>This page describes the chemical cleaning of metallic ashes (calx) using corrosives and a specialized "bearded vessel" to strip away foul-smelling earthiness and excess sulfur. The goal is to reach a pure, stable "body" that is colored either white or red, depending on the metal's nature.</summary>

# ON INVESTIGATION <note>original: "DE INVESTIGATIONE"</note>

[Meth]od <note>original: "dus", completing "mo-dus" from the previous page</note> this is: First, these bodies <term>bodies: the physical metals being worked upon</term> having been purged and, as was said, reduced <note>returned to a metallic state after being in a powder or "calx"</note>, are then calcined <term>calcined: roasted into a fine powder or ash</term> once again with fire and with the previously mentioned cleansing aids. Then, they are to be dissolved with those substances which are solvents.

For this "water" <note>the liquid resulting from the dissolution of the metal</note> is our stone, and it is quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury, the fluid principle of alchemy</term> derived from quicksilver, and sulfur derived from sulfur; it is a body made spiritual, refined, and thinned. This can be further improved by strengthening its elementary virtues <note>the four qualities of hot, cold, wet, and dry</note> with other prepared substances that come from the same lineage of its own kind. 

By this means, one increases the color, the fixation <term>fixation: the quality of being "fixed" or stable in fire without evaporating</term>, the weight, the purity, the meltability, and all other properties that belong to the perfect elixir. And this is the method, investigated by us alone, for the preparation, purification, refinement, and improvement of mineral bodies in general.

### *The Specific Preparation of Jupiter <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term>.*

Jupiter is prepared in many ways, but most effectively through this method. Let it be placed in a calcination furnace <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature, but below its melting point, to bring about thermal decomposition or remove impurities</term> in a vessel suited for this purpose, and let a fire be built beneath it until the body itself melts. By then stirring the liquefied body with a perforated iron spatula and drawing off the dross <note>original: "scoriam," the scum or impurities that rise to the surface</note> which forms upon it, and by stirring it while the heat of the fire remains constant, a good quantity of powder will eventually gather on the surface. Let that powder then be set aside, and let the metal be stirred again, until by alternating turns through frequent motion—or by the removal of the dross—it has gathered a good quantity as before; this too is then set aside. And so, turn by turn, continue until the entire body is reduced into a very fine powder. 

That powder should then be sifted and placed back into the furnace, giving it a good heat; however, the heat must not exceed the point of melting <note>original: "fusionem," the temperature at which the powder would turn back into a liquid mass</note>, and it must be stirred many times. Let it stand in the fire of its calcination in this way for a natural day <term>natural day: a full 24-hour period</term>, or until about

Let the plates be replaced with new salt; and in this alternating fashion let them be calcined until all the plates have been consumed or corroded by the benefit of the salt and fire: because the salt cor- <note>original: "cor-", the text breaks mid-word, likely "corrodit" (corrodes), to be continued on the next page</note>

### *On the preparation of Mars.*

**Mars** <note>original: "Mars" — the alchemical name for Iron</note> is also better prepared by this method. ### OF THE MASTERWORK

[prepara]tion, <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "preparation" from the previous page.</note> just as you did with **Sol** <note>original: "Sole" — the alchemical name for Gold</note>. And the water of dissolved **Luna** <note>original: "Lunae" — the alchemical name for Silver</note> is the **ferment** <term>fermentum: an agent of transformation, like yeast in dough, that allows the elixir to "multiply" its power</term> prepared for the white spiritual **elixir** <term>elixir: the legendary substance capable of transmuting metals or curing illness</term>.

And let it be fixed with that which is in it of the **fixative** substance; and let it not be burned by those things which do not burn **gold** and **silver**; and let it bring about solidifications and weight with proper and perfect firing <note>original: "ignitione" — the application of heat or fire</note>.

# 500 ON THE INVESTIGATION

Yet you should not understand "short time" to mean
that it can be constructed in a few days or hours 
the first time. Rather, it is because, 
compared to other modern truth-tellers,
and even compared to the operations of nature, the 
truth <note>The "truth" here refers to the successful completion of the alchemical work.</note> is finished more quickly in this way. 
Hence the philosophers say: **The Medicine** <term>Medicina: in alchemy, this refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, which "heals" imperfect metals into gold</term> **is that for which a long space of time has been anticipated.** <note>original: "Medicina est, cuius longum tempus spacium anticipauit" — meaning the alchemical process accelerates a perfection that would take nature ages to achieve.</note>
Therefore, I tell you to endure 
patiently, lest you perhaps 
shorten the delay; for **haste is something from 
the devil.** <note>original: "festinantia quædam ex parte diaboli est" — a famous medieval proverb often used to warn against cutting corners in spiritual or scientific work.</note> 
Therefore, he who does not have patience, 
let him stay his hand from the work: 
because the belief in haste will hinder him. 
Every natural action has its own
movement and determined time,
in which it is finished in a greater or lesser space. 
For these things, three are necessary: 
patience, delay, and the fitting of instruments. 
Concerning these, and concerning their 
method of acting, we have sufficiently addressed the 
artisan in the summary of our **perfect mastery** <term>magisterij: the "Magistery" or the total knowledge and practice required to complete the Great Work</term>
in various chapters: 
in which it can be tested whether he has been
