<meta>This page continues the conclusion of a discussion regarding the time required for the alchemical work, transitioning into a definitive statement on the composition of the "Stone" and the author's reliance on practical experience over mere theory.</meta>

->### OF THE MASTERY<-

[mani]fest proof and open reasoning, we conclude: that our **Stone** <term>Lapis: the Philosopher’s Stone, the legendary substance sought by alchemists to transmute base metals into gold and achieve spiritual perfection</term> is nothing other than a **stinking spirit** <note>original: "spiritum foetentem" — this likely refers to the pungent sulfurous vapors released during the heating of the primary materials.</note> and **living water**, which we have also named **dry water** <note>original: "aquam vivam... siccam aquam" — a classic alchemical paradox used to describe substances like quicksilver (mercury) that flow like water but do not wet the hands.</note>; and [it is created] through natural **decoction** <term>decoctionem: a process of prolonged heating or "cooking" a substance to refine and concentrate its essence</term>, and through a true proportion of the cleansing and uniting [elements] in such a union that nothing can be added to it or taken away. To these, a third thing must be added to shorten the work: that is, the **perfect body** <note>The "perfect body" usually refers to gold or silver, added to the mixture to act as a "ferment" or template for the desired transformation.</note> made thin.

From these premises, therefore, those things are clear in which the truth is near, and which perfect the body itself. Indeed, by our investigation—of which we are certain—and through sure experience, we clearly consider all those things to be true which have now been written by us for you alone, according to what we have perceived through the reasoning of our mind in these volumes. 

However, those things which we have extracted with our fingers through experience, seen with our eyes, and touched with our hands, we have written in our **summary of the perfection of mastery** <term>magisterij: the "Magistery," or the complete set of operations required to attain the Great Work</term>. Therefore, let the wise artisan study our volumes, gathering together our true intention which is scattered throughout—

<meta>catchword: -n; (the beginning of the word "intentionem" continued on the next page)</meta>

<summary>The author defines the Philosopher's Stone as a union of "stinking spirit" and "dry water," emphasizing that his instructions are based on physical, hands-on experimentation rather than just theory. He advises the student to look for his "true intention," which he has intentionally scattered throughout his writings.</summary>

<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, stinking spirit, dry water, decoction, perfect body, experimental method, mastery, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 322 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's cautionary advice, transitioning from personal experience to the theoretical and material foundations of the alchemical art, specifically the roles of "perfect" and "imperfect" bodies.</meta>

->### ON INVESTIGATION<-

[reason]ing; which we have proposed in various places lest it be made public to the malicious or the ignorant. Let the seeker test what has been gathered until, by studying and experimenting with the persistence of ingenious labor, he arrives at a total understanding. Therefore, let the craftsman exercise himself and discover it. We now present the mode of this investigation, our inner heart being moved by love <note>original: "commotis amore visceribus" — literally "the bowels moved by love," a common medieval expression for deep, sincere emotion or compassion</note>, from which we also, through our own consideration, have extracted a clear knowledge of the truth. 

For we consider that this science treats of **perfect** and **imperfect bodies** <note>In the alchemical tradition, "perfect bodies" refer to the noble metals, gold and silver, which do not corrode. "Imperfect bodies" are base metals like lead, copper, or tin.</note>. It also grants full knowledge of the process of perfecting, as well as the corruption of **matter and form** <term>materia et forma: these are Aristotelian terms where "matter" is the raw physical substance and "form" is the essential nature or organizing principle that gives the substance its specific properties</term>. 

We consider our investigation to show that the **matter** of the perfect bodies—along with the form and root of their mixing—is pure and free from any subsequent corruption until the final completion of the work. We have also considered, from the opposite perspective, that the substance of both the imperfect and the perfect bodies is one and the same everywhere: namely, **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum" — mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" or liquid principle of all metals</note> and **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur" — considered the "father" or active, combustible principle of metals</note>. We find that these two, before they are mixed, are the cause of the corruption of the imperfect bodies, 

<meta>catchword: acci-</meta>

<summary>The author explains that all metals, whether noble or base, share a common internal substance composed of mercury and sulfur. He emphasizes that the secret of alchemy lies in understanding how to manage these substances through persistent study and "ingenious labor."</summary>
<keywords>perfect bodies, imperfect bodies, matter and form, quicksilver, sulfur, alchemical theory, mercury-sulfur theory</keywords>

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page concludes the current treatise, following the discussion on the shared "matter" of all metals (mercury). It addresses how impurities are merely "accidental" additions to base metals and can be removed through the alchemical art to reveal a purity that rivals or exceeds natural gold.</meta>

# OF THE MASTERY <note>original: "MAGISTERII" — referring to the Magisterium or the "Great Work" of alchemical transformation.</note>

[to have] supervened accidentally <note>Continuing from the previous page, the author refers to how impurities "accidentally" (meaning incidentally, not essentially) attached themselves to the base metals.</note>: which gave a new and corrupt **form** to the **matter**. For when we have seen **imperfect bodies** <term>imperfecta corpora: base metals like lead, tin, or copper, which alchemists believed were "diseased" or incomplete versions of gold</term>—through our experience and through our ingenuity—prepared and cleansed from all superfluous corruption, whether it be fugitive <note>original: "fugitiva" — referring to volatile substances that evaporate or "flee" when heated</note> impurity or settled earthiness; we found them to possess greater clarity, brilliance, and purity than those bodies that are naturally **perfect** <note>Gold and silver were considered "perfect" because they do not corrode in fire or air.</note>. 

Through this kind of consideration, we have reached the perfect and complete end of this **science**; which we have written perfectly and fully in our volumes. Therefore, study those works, and you will find our entire science, which we have condensed from the books of the ancients.

->FINIS.<-

<summary>This concluding page asserts that alchemical purification can make base metals even purer than naturally occurring gold and silver, ending the treatise with an invitation to study the author's other collected works.</summary>
